#### Unit Project

1. Students may choose their own fairy tales to talk about.

An example might be the fairy tale "Little Red Riding Hood." The story has gone through a number of revisions so that it is fitting for children. When we read it as children, we are taught the moral that one should never trust strangers, but when we read it as adults, especially as trained students of English, we can see some other themes even without going to the original versions, for example, natural cycles, rite of passage, and erotic connotations.

2. Students may choose their own love stories to rewrite. It is important that the rewriting shows their idea of love.

---

# Unit 5

## Text A · The Green Banana · Donald Batchelder

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

**1. Multiculturalism vs ethnocentrism**

**Multiculturalism:** a situation in which all the different cultural or racial groups in a society have equal rights and opportunities, and none is ignored or regarded as unimportant. (Collins COBUILD Advanced Dictionary of American English, 2007)

**Ethnocentrism:** the belief that one particular race or nationality of people is superior to all others. (Collins COBUILD Advanced Dictionary of American English, 2007)

These two concepts may help us to understand the thesis of the essay, namely the point or message the writer intends to get across to the reader: all nations/cultures have treasures to share with others and people can benefit from these treasures only if they shake off the belief that their own is superior to all others.

**2. Cooling system of an automobile engine**

To help students understand the wonderful use of the green banana, it is advisable to supply them with sufficient information about the basic systems of a car engine in the briefest possible way.

Four systems of a car engine:

**Combustion** (燃烧系统), which turns fuel into the power that pushes the car

**Lubricating** (润滑系统), which reduces the friction (摩擦) produced by the engine's moving parts

**Electrical** (电力系统), which manages the electricity necessary to keep the engine running

**Cooling** (冷却系统), which draws heat away from the engine block. This system consists of a radiator (散热器), a water pump, water jackets (隔热罩) and a fan.

#### 2 Structure

The text can be roughly divided into three parts, the first two of which can be further divided into several sections.

**Part I The author's interesting experience in a mountain village in central Brazil.**

(paras. 1–4)

1. The radiator of his jeep began to leak. The writer stopped at a village, looking for help, and a man fixed it easily with green bananas. (para. 1)

2. The man told the author that everybody in their village knew the leak could be stopped by green bananas. He also told him everybody around there knew that the high rock near their village marked the very center of the world. (paras. 2–4)

**Part II The author's reflections on his experience (paras. 5–7)**

1. He appreciated the local people's genius in their use of the green banana to stop leaks, which is unknown to him although he is a product of American education. (para. 5)

2. He came to understand that the villagers' claim about the rock being the center of the world is no different from that of other people, his own included. We all regard the place where we are known and where we know others as the center of the world. (paras. 6–7)

**Part III The author's conclusion: Each culture is full of things like the green banana with special value and meaning, waiting for people from the outside to encounter: (para. 8)**

#### 3 Teaching Tips

#### 1. Retelling

Retelling is a good way of reinforcing what the students have learned about a text besides helping develop their logical thinking. The first part of the text is good for retelling. The students can also be encouraged to talk about similar experiences of their own. Here are a few topics for such oral practice.

■ Describe the writer's two learning moments.

■ Do you agree with the writer that every nation or community has treasures to offer to the rest of the world? Give an example.

■ What's your understanding of learning moments? Describe one of such moments you have experienced.

#### 2. Further discussion

1) We cannot expect an in-depth discussion of the second part of the essay as the students' English is limited at this stage. However, we should still give the students a couple of thought-provoking questions, even though the discussion might be very superficial, for this is useful training.

2) Here are a few topics for discussion.

■ Do you agree with the writer's conclusion about the center of the world?

■ Do you think people who come from different cultures are unlikely to understand each other?

■ Are there cultural conflicts between students from different regions? If so, how do you settle the conflicts?

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

1. Although it might have happened anywhere, my encounter with the green banana... (para. 1)

**might have happened:** used to describe what was possible but did not happen in the past

**encounter:** a particular type of experience; an occasion when you deal with or experience sth

eg It was his first encounter with cigarettes.

The man described his close encounter with a bear in the forest.

2. My ancient jeep was straining up through beautiful countryside when the radiator began to leak. (para. 1)

My old jeep was driving with great difficulty up the mountain road, and suddenly the radiator began to leak.

**ancient:** very old (used humorously here)

**strain:** to try very hard

*More examples:*

The old man was straining to open the jar.

People strained to hear what the speaker was saying.

The verb can also be used as a transitive verb.

eg I strained my ears to hear what they were whispering.

I strained my eyes in the darkness, but saw nothing.

**when:** used to say what was happening or had just happened when a particular event happened

*More examples:*

I was daydreaming when the teacher told me to answer a question.

They had just arrived home when the telephone rang.

**radiator:** a device, usually a container filled with water, that sends out heat, often as part of a heating or cooling system

3. ... I was ten miles from the nearest mechanic. (para. 1)

**mechanic:** a person who repairs machines (such as car engines)

4. The over-heated engine forced me to stop at the next village, which consisted of a small store and a few houses that were scattered here and there. (para. 1)

**over-heated:** too hot. The prefix "over-" here means "too much".

*More examples:*

over-lighted, overdressed, overeat, oversleep, overkill, overload, overcharge, overvalue

*More collocations:*

over-heated economy, an over-heated room, over-heated discussion, etc

**consist of:** to be formed or made up of (specific things or people)

eg Lunch consisted of rice, vegetables and fish yesterday.

The team consists of twenty males and four females.

**scattered here and there:** spread over a wide range in different places, not very close to one another

5. They could see three fine streams of hot water spouting from holes in the jacket of the radiator. (para. 1)

**fine:** very thin or in very small pieces or drops

eg fine hair, thread, sand, etc

**stream of hot water:** continuous flow of hot water

cf stream of cars (para. 18, Unit 1)

*More examples:*

stream of traffic, stream of visitors, etc

**the jacket of the radiator:** (发动机的) 散热器的隔热罩

6. He sent a boy running for some green bananas. (para. 1)

He told a boy to go and fetch some green bananas.

**send sb doing sth:** to cause sb to do sth; to cause sth to happen

eg The movie sent many people crying.

The icy road sent several people slipping and falling.

The news sent the company's shares dropping by 3%.

7. He patted me on the shoulder, assuring me that everything would work out. (para. 1)

**pat me on the shoulder:** to pat my shoulder

Similarly, we say "to wound sb in the leg", "to hit sb in the back", etc.

**assure sb that:** to tell sb that sth is sure to happen or is definitely true

*More examples:*

I can assure you that he will be all right.

The mechanic assured me that the car would be good for another five years.

**work out:** (of a problem or difficult situation) to gradually get better or get solved

eg Don't worry, things will work out in the end.

8. I did not ask them, though, as that would show my ignorance. (para. 2)

Although I did not know what they would do to my radiator, I did not ask them because that would show my lack of knowledge about the use of green bananas.

**though:** (adv) despite the fact that...

eg We went to the same high school; we were not close friends though.

*Note:* The difference between "although" and "though" is that the former is always used as a conjunction, whereas the latter can also be used as an adverb, and as such can be placed at the end of a sentence or clause.

9. Huge rock formation, like Sugar Loaf in Rio... (para. 2)

**rock formation:** rocks formed in unusual shapes

**Sugar Loaf in Rio:** Until the 19th century, household sugar usually took the form of a loaf, oblong and rounded at the top. Here it refers to a mountain, which looks like a sugar loaf.

10. I looked to see if he was teasing me... (para. 3)

I looked (at him) in order to find out whether he was joking...

11. He, in turn, inspected me carefully, as if to make sure I grasped the significance of his statement. (para. 3)

(I looked at him in surprise,) as a result, he looked at me closely, as if he wanted to make sure that I understood the importance of what he had said.

**in turn:** as a result

*More examples:*

Our parents take care of us when we are young. And we in turn should take care of them when they are old.

Each generation will in turn deal with its own problems.

**as if to make sure...:** an elliptical adverbial clause of manner, the missing part is "he wanted" (the subject and main verb of the clause).

*More examples:*

She cleared her throat as if to speak.

He stood up as if to leave.

"As if" can also be followed by a present participle.

eg He was standing by the window as if waiting for somebody. (=... as if he was waiting for someone)

Generally, "as if" is used with a complete clause.

eg He looked as if he had seen a ghost.

12. The occasion called for some show of recognition on my part. (para. 3)

**call for:** to need or require

*More examples:*

Those poor areas call for our special attention.

I don't think the situation calls for any alarm.

**on sb's part on the part of sb:** by or from sb

*More examples:*

The project calls for hard work on everyone's part.

It was a good effort on the part of all the students.

**recognition:** agreement that sth is true or important

eg The teacher's smile is recognition of our effort.

The new republic is trying hard to achieve international recognition.

13. ... trying to show interest if not complete acceptance. (para. 3)

... trying to show some interest even though I did not quite agree with him.

**if not:** used to introduce an even stronger alternative to what has just been said

eg The rescue team saved hundreds of lives if not thousands.

The little boy runs as fast as his elder brother if not (even) faster.

14. an armful of green bananas (para. 4)

**armful:** a noun, meaning the amount of sth that a person can carry in one or both arms

eg an armful of books/roses/clothes

*Similar collocations:*

a handful of coins, a spoonful of sugar, a roomful of young people, etc

*Note:* The "-ful" in these words is a noun suffix (名词后缀).

cf adjective suffix (形容词后缀) "-ful", as in careful, hopeful, respectful, etc

15. ... pressed the cut end against the radiator jacket. (para. 3)

**cut:** past participle used as attributive modifier

*More examples:*

a heated discussion, a forced smile, spoken English, a broken car, a marked place, an astonished look, educated people, an experienced doctor, used books, a torn jacket, married people, clenched fists, wounded soldiers, the tied end, pressed hair, a calculated move

16. I was so astonished at this that I must have looked rather foolish. (para. 4)

**be astonished at:** to be very surprised. "At" here is used to show the thing that causes the feeling.

*Similar expressions:*

be amazed at, be alarmed at, be mad at, etc

**must have done:** used to say that a person believes that he or someone else has done sth in the past, but is not absolutely sure

*More examples:*

I must have left my mobile phone at home. I can't find it in my bag.

You must have done a lot of research. Your presentation is wonderful.

17. ... give me extra bananas to take along in case my radiator should give me trouble again. (para. 4)

... give me more bananas to take with me so that I would be able to deal with it if my radiator should leak again.

**in case:** a conjunction used to introduce a reason clause indicating a possible future situation which is your reason for doing sth

*More examples:*

Take a raincoat in case it rains.

I gave him a call in case he didn't know.

They decided to search the car in case there should be a bomb.

*Note:* "In case" is followed by the simple present, simple past tenses, or "should".

**should:** used to say that you believe sth is likely to happen in the future

*More examples:*

You can always come to me if you should need help.

Say that you don't know if they should ask.

18. As a product of American education, I have never paid the slightest attention to the green banana, except to regard it as a fruit whose time had not yet come. (para. 5)

As I have been brought up and educated in the US I knew little about the uses of the green banana. The only thing I knew about them is that they are not ripened, ready to eat as fruit. (The writer implies that his education had not taught him much about other cultures or prepared him to discover the genius of other nations/cultures.)

**be a/the product of sth (a particular background or experience):** (of a person or his quality) to be typical of that background or the result of that experience

eg My father is a product of his times—the 1960s. (=My father is like other people who grew up in the 1960s.)

His good manners are the product of a good upbringing.

cf **be a/the product of sth:** (of sth) to be the result of sth

eg The book is the product of three years of research.

The place is only the product of the poet's imagination.

**except:** As a preparation, "except" can be used in front of a to-infinitive after such verbs as know, say, speak, talk, etc.

*More examples:*

Jane would not speak to his brother except to answer questions.

The Nightingale asked nothing of the Student except to be a true lover.

But after the verb "do", an infinitive without "to" is required.

*More Examples:*

There was little we could do except wait.

He did nothing except make empty promises.

19. Suddenly, on that mountain road, its time had come to meet my need. (para. 5)

Suddenly, when I badly needed help on that mountain road the green banana stopped the leaks of my radiator jacket, and thus came to my attention.

**to meet my need:** an attributive qualifier (定语) modifying "its time"

20. It was my own time that had not come, all in relation to it. (para. 5)

It required the effort on my own part to learn about the green banana and everything connected with it. (This means if people want to learn about the treasures of other cultures, they have to go and discover them. Only then will they come to your notice and benefit you.)

21. I had been wondering for some time about what educators like to call "learning moments", and I now knew I had just experienced two of them at once. (para. 5)

For some time I had been thinking about what educators mean by "learning moments", and now that I had experienced two of such moments at the same time, I knew what they mean. The two learning moments the writer refers to are: 1) the moment when he realized that every culture/nation has wonderful treasures to share with others; 2) the moment when he came to see every village, town, region or country has a right to regard itself as the center of the world. Pay attention to the structure of the object clause "what educators like to call 'learning moments'". "educators": subject; "like to call": main verb; "learning moment": object; "what": object complement (宾语的补语)

Ask the students to explain "learning moment" in their own words or using an anecdote.

*More examples:*

what you call rags 你称为破衣烂衫之物

what you call treasures 你称为珍宝之物

**at once:** together, at the same time

*More examples:*

I'd like to help, but I can't do two things at once.

The children all started talking at once, and I couldn't hear any of them.

22. It took me a little longer to fully grasp the importance of the rock which the villagers believed marked the center of the world. (para. 6)

Obviously, it is easier to see the genius of the local people in their wonderful use of the green banana

to stop leaks than it is to accept their belief that a particular rock in the area is the center of the world.

**the villagers believed:** a parenthesis (限定性的插入语), without which the structure is intact,

but the meaning would be different. The sentence would mean that the writer believed the rock marked the center of the world.

23. I had at first doubted their claim, as I knew for a fact that the center was located somewhere else in New England. (para. 6)

The writer obviously was born and brought up in New England. Therefore he regarded it as the center of the world.

Notice the humor in the mention of his grandfather just like that mechanic in Brazil.

**know for a fact:** to be sure, to believe that sth is true

*More examples:*

I know for a fact that he did it.

I know for a fact that he's older than my parents.

24. We all tend to regard as the center that special place where we are known, ... (para. 6)

This is often the source of provincialism, regionalism, chauvinism, racism and imperialism. The existing ethnocentrism calls for a special warning. Unless people are all ready to understand and respect other cultures, the world will not live in peace.

Note the inversion: the object of regard—"that special place..." is too long that it is placed after the object complement "as the center". The normal order is: ... to regard that special place... as the center.

25. But once a conscious breakthrough to a second center is made, a life-long perspective and collection can begin. (para. 7)

But once you make a serious effort to overcome your prejudice and go out to encounter different cultures, the rest of your life your mind will keep broadening as you experience and benefit from more and more treasures of other cultures/nations.

26. The cultures of the world are full of unexpected green bananas with special value and meaning. (para. 8)

Here the green bananas have become a symbol of hidden treasures from every culture. For proper understanding of a piece of writing, it is often important to notice such symbolic language and to know what the symbols stand for. Symbol is a sign, shape or object which is used to represent sth else.

eg the heart shape: a symbol of love

the flag: a symbol of one's country

the lion: a symbol of courage

### III Translation of Text A

青香蕉

多纳德·巴切尔德

也许这样的事在哪儿都会发生，但我同青香蕉的邂逅却是从巴西腹地的一条陡峭的山路上开始的。当时，我那辆老掉牙的吉普正费劲地行驶在景色秀丽的山间，突然散热器开始漏水了。最近的维修店在十里之外，引擎太热，我不得不赶紧停车，在附近的一个村庄停了下来。村子里有一家小杂货店，还有几栋房屋零星散布在周围。很快就有村民来看出了什么事，只见三股细细的热水流从散热器外罩上的三个小洞里涌了出来。"这好办，"一个人说道。他吩咐一个小男孩赶紧取些青香蕉来，他拍拍我的肩膀说没问题，让我放心。"用青香蕉就行，"他笑着说，其他的人也都纷纷附和。

我和村民们闲聊了起来，我一边聊一边却琢磨这青香蕉到底怎么修散热器。不过我向来没想过问他们，那会表明我的无知，因此我就硬着头皮不问，尽管心里迫切想知道答案。我试探着问正在使劲干活的一个人，那高高耸立着的青岩石是不是远处的什么特别的山。旁边还有一座高耸的青山。我问他是不是那座岩石标志着世界的中心。"世界的中心？"我重复着他的话，尽管心里并不那么认同，也竭力表现出感兴趣的样子。他点点头，"绝对的。"我周围的人都说。

就在这时，小男孩抱着满怀的青香蕉回来了。男人把一个青香蕉切成两段，把切面那端按在散热器的外罩上，香蕉在滚热的金属外罩上很快就化成了一个黏糊糊的团块，一下子就把漏洞都给堵上了。我看得目瞪口呆，样子肯定很傻，周围的人都笑了起来。然后他们给我的散热器装满了水，又让我带上几根香蕉在路上用，以防散热器再出故障。路上，我又用青香蕉补了一次洞，一小时后我就又出发了，把车开到了目的地。那里的修理店老板问："谁教你用青香蕉的？"我告诉他说："一个村民。"他又问："他有没有告诉你那个标记着世界中心的岩石？"他又说："我父亲就是那儿的人。"他又说："那是我所确信的世界的中心。附近人人都知道这事儿。"

由于我深受美国教育影响，我从来就没注意过青香蕉，它们对我来说只不过是一种尚未到季的水果。可是那天在那条山路上它却突然出现在我的面前，解决了我的困难。可是仔细想想，其实青香蕉早就存在了，早在香蕉起源之日就已经存在了，那个村子里的人早就知道它的用途，只是我以前一直没有机会了解到这一点。现在我开始认识到那些村民特殊的聪明才智，体验到香蕉的妙用。一段时间以来我一直在思考教育工作者习以为常的"顿悟"概念到底是什么，现在，我终于明白了，我刚刚体验了两次这样的"顿悟"。

关于周围的那座山峰是世界的中心标志之说，我对这其真正的顿悟的领悟来得稍微慢一些。起初我并不认同村民们的说法，因为我确信世界的中心在新英格兰的某个地方。毕竟，那是祖父出生的地方。后来我逐渐认识到村民们的看法颇有道理，并且赞同他们的这种认识。我们都会把某个特殊的地方视为中心，因为在那个地方我们最为人所知，我们也认识其他人，那里的一切对我们都有非凡的意义，在那里我们知道自己是谁，活得也有意义。因此无论是家庭，还是学校，城镇还是其他地区，那都有可能是我们心中的世界中心。

那天夜里我逐渐明白了一个其实非常简单的道理：每个地方对生活在那里的人来说都有着特殊的意义，从某种意义上说，每个地方都是世界的中心。世界上有无数个这样的中心，任何一个学者、旅行者或其个人来都不太可能体验所有这些中心。但是，一旦你努力冲破自己的中心去体验另一个中心，你的视野就会不断地扩展，你将终身受益。

在世界文化之林中，到处都有你意想不到的青香蕉，个个都有其独特的价值与意义。它们历史源远流长，慢慢地成熟，或许正耐心地等待着人们去摘取。事实上，假如我们愿意离开各自心中的中心去体验新的地方，肯定会有一只青香蕉在等待着你

们每个人。

### IV Key to Exercises

**Lead-in**

1. Possible talking points:

Benefits:

➢ Experience new things and make friends with people from other cultures

➢ Gain some basic understanding of other cultures and the world as a whole

➢ Gain better understanding of one's own culture

➢ Understand different ways of thinking and different perspectives

➢ Learn to understand and evaluate fairly and suspend judgment

Problems:

➢ Inefficient communication because of language barriers

➢ Misunderstandings, embarrassments, or even conflicts due to cultural differences

➢ Ethnocentrism (the tendency to regard one's own culture as the one and only criterion for judgment)

➢ Failure to comprehend the intricacies of a multi-cultural world

2. Possible talking points:

➢ Respect all cultures

➢ Try to acquire a proficient command of foreign languages

➢ Try to learn foreign cultures through learning the languages as well as their literature, history, etc.

➢ Try to have a profound understanding of the Chinese culture and its differences as well as commonalities with other cultures

➢ Try to see ourselves as a member of the global community and learn to think in others' perspectives

➢ Be prepared for possible misunderstandings, embarrassments, or even conflicts in cross-cultural communication and learn not to judge too hastily

**Language Focus**

1

1. mechanic, traveler, educator: suffixes indicating a person who does a certain job or activity

2. ignorance, significance, importance, acceptance, recognition, destination, education, attention, relation, collection, meaning, statement: suffixes indicating a quality, action or thing relating directly to a certain verb

3. armful: suffix indicating measurement

2

1. agree with, call for, consist of, cut in half, dawn on sb, melt into, point to, reflect on, regard as, rise up, take along, talk about, work out

2. after all, all along, (two) at once, in a sense, in case, in fact, in relation to, in turn, on

## （续）

one's part

3. an armful of, once more, right over there

**3**

appreciate the special genius, doubt one's claim, encounter/experience the learning moments, fix the car, grasp the significance, make a breakthrough, mark/represent the center of the world, meet one's need, reach the destination, refill one's radiator, show ignorance, stop the leaks

**4**

1. My ancient jeep was straining up through beautiful countryside when the radiator began to leak. (para. 1)

   ... main verb (past continuous/past perfect tense) + when + time clause

2. I was so astonished at this that I must have looked rather foolish and everyone laughed. (para. 4)

   ... so + adj./adv. + that + result clause

3. They... gave me extra bananas to take along in case my radiator should give me trouble again. (para. 4)

   ... (just) in case + reason clause ... (should) do/did

4. He patted me on the shoulder... (para. 1)

5. He sent a boy running for some green bananas. (para. 1)

**Reading Comprehension**

**1**

**Part I** The author's interesting experience in a mountain village in central Brazil. (paras. 1-4)

1. The radiator of his jeep began to leak. The writer stopped at a village, looking for help, and a man fixed it easily with green bananas.

2. The man told the author that everybody in their village knew the leak could be stopped by green bananas. He also told him everybody around there knew that the high rock near their village marked the very center of the world.

**Part II** The author's reflections on his experience (paras. 5-7)

1. He appreciated the local people's genius in their use of the green banana to stop leaks, which is unknown to him although he is a product of American education.

2. He came to understand that the villagers' claim about the rock being the center of the world is no different from that of other people, his own included. We all regard the place where we are known and where we know others as the center of the world.

**Part III** The author's conclusion (para. 8)

Each culture is full of things like the green banana with special value and meaning, waiting for people from the outside to encounter.

**2**

1. The story I am going to tell could have happened anywhere, but the way it happened in my case was quite interesting. First, it happened in central Brazil. Second, I was driving an ancient jeep and straining up a steep mountain road when the radiator began to leak suddenly. I found myself in a pretty desperate situation.

2. The man was friendly and helpful. Therefore, when he told me about the rock being the center of the world, I could not say he was wrong. But out of politeness, I at least needed to show I was listening to him with interest.

3. As someone educated in the United States, I naturally had never paid any attention to green bananas, which to me simply meant a fruit not yet ready to be eaten.

4. But as soon as you can make an effort to understand that yours is not the only center of the world, your eyes will open wider and wider, and you will look at the world in a new way and begin to understand, respect, and learn more and more from other cultures throughout your life.

**3**

1. The story is about how an American traveler had his leaking jeep fixed with green bananas by the local villagers in central Brazil and how he came to appreciate the hidden treasures of different cultures. The title can be interpreted as a pun: it refers to both bananas that have not yet ripened and the unknown treasures of different cultures.

2. The author is an American traveler whose hometown is somewhere in New England, in the northeastern part of America. He is educated. He didn't know green bananas could be used as a glue because he had never been taught that, he had always seen the green banana as a fruit which has not yet ripened. No matter how well-educated people may be, they are educated in a specific country and are taught the things taken for granted or considered important by a specific culture; they may not be aware of things common to other cultures. Besides, they may consider their culture "right" or "superior" and may have never bothered to know other cultures.

3. The man told the author that the rock marked the center of the world. He was so sure because all people around there, their forefathers included, believed so. The author didn't believe him, but he tried to show some interest. He didn't contradict the villager because it would be rude to do so, especially when they had just helped him with his jeep and he was alone among strangers in a place far away from home. He thought the center of the world was somewhere in New England. I think they used the term "the center of the world" in a slightly different sense. The villagers used it to refer to both the geographical center of the world and the place where they have both identity and meaning, while the author only used in to refer to the latter sense.

4. I agree with the author's idea of the center of the world. I don't think there is a center of the world geographically accepted universally, but everybody has some special place that means a lot to them, some kind of harbor or anchor where they form their identity, become members of communities, and accept some set of values and norms. That's why I agree that in a sense there are numerous centers of the world. Such centers are defined by how a person relates to it, not by its geographical position. I agree that it is important to realize this, because this way we will not be trapped in our own centers and our eyes will be opened to other centers. We can have a better understanding of others as well as ourselves. Besides, to regard one's own center as the only one in the world and turn a blind eye to other centers is often the source of provincialism, regionalism, chauvinism, racism and imperialism.

5. "Learning moments" are moments when new experiences lead to new knowledge and growth. The two learning moments the writer refers to are: 1) the moment when he realized that every culture/nation has wonderful treasures to share with others; 2) the moment when he came to see every village, town, region or country has a right to regard itself as the center of the world. If we step out of our center of the world, we may discover wonderful cultural treasures and have a fair and more profound understanding of other cultures just as the author did.

6. It is not easy to do that, because we are inevitably biased against other cultures if we have only experienced one center. In order to experience other centers, we have to first of all be aware of the multitude of centers of the world, and then step out of our own small center to broaden both our minds and our experiences. "A conscious breakthrough" means to make a serious effort to overcome our prejudice and go out to encounter different cultures. We must be conscious of first of all the danger of being confined to one center, including prejudice, hubris, provincialism or even ethnocentrism, and secondly, the fact that all centers of the world deserve equal recognition and respect.

7. Sample answer:

I used to regard my family as my center of the world, the place where I spent most of my formative years, where my loved ones are, and where I always feel most understood. After reading the text, however, my idea of the center of the world has changed. I find it hard to define my center of the world, partly because I am still in the process of finding my identity under multiple influences — family, school, online community, etc., and partly because I feel the center of the world may not be a specific place, especially now that I am aware of what the "center of the world" means. Of course in a general sense, I consider China and the Chinese culture my center of the world, because being a Chinese is the essence of my identity and my cultural affinity is always with China.

1. To be or not to be (infinitive phrase used as the subject)

生存还是毁灭，这是一个问题。

2. (eat) to live; (live) to eat (infinitive phrase used as an adverbial of purpose)

我们吃东西是为了活着，但活着不是为了吃。

3. to go to the nursing home (object of "want"); to see Grandma (adverbial of purpose); to go with her (direct object); to do (attributive); to visit Grandma (subject); to finish my homework (attributive)

我妈要去养老院看我姥姥，想让我跟她一起去。我告诉她我有很多家庭作业要做，可是她说看姥姥花不了多少时间，我还有足够的时间完成作业。

4. to live and let live (predicative)

我认为国际关系中最好的政策仍然是和平共存。

5. to be threatened to be (adverbial of result)

这就会像东郭先生和狼的故事一样。你出于好心放了狼，结果却受到威胁，要被当做这头恶狼的晚餐。

6. to hurt somebody (adverbial of purpose); to drop it (adverbial of result)

经常有人捡起石头想伤人，却砸了自己的脚。

7. to prove himself to be right (attributive); to flatter someone (attributive); to tell him (predicative)

人性的弱点之一是无法抗拒地想证明自己是对的，从不犯错，因此奉承他人最可靠的方法就是告诉他，他是你见过的唯一讨厌奉承的人。

8. To tell the truth (adverbial compliment, usually placed at the beginning of a sentence); to conquer poverty/to conquer wealth (subject, "it" is used as the logical subject); see many people suffer (part of the complex object)

说实话说，我认为战胜贫困比战胜财富容易。我见过很多人受苦，不是因为他们太穷了，而是因为他们太富了。

9. to eat and sleep (adverbial of purpose)

我一路从东海岸开到西海岸，只停下来吃饭睡觉。

**Unit Project**

1. Tips for students:

Students can follow the example of the text in structuring their speech. Start with the story of the cultural shock, add a little bit of fun and suspense, and proceed to talk about their learning moments.

2. Possible talking points:

- The premise in dealing with different cultures is to understand and respect all cultures.
- Globalization does not mean all cultures in the world should become the same, or that there is one cultural model that applies to all cultures.
- When cultures interact, they inevitably learn and adopt ideas from each other, as history has proved. This doesn't mean that they will give up their own ideas or values or even their identity, rather they integrate such ideas or values into their own.
- Cultural are always evolving, and interactions with others culture is an important way.
- Interactions between cultures are mutual and should not be forced.

---

# Unit 6

## Text A · After Twenty Years · O. Henry

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

**1. Theme**

Theme: the main subject or idea of a work of literature. The theme of this story is a common one—loyalty to a friend vs devotion to public duty. Bob and Jimmy had obviously been close friends twenty years before when they were young. Otherwise they would not have made this appointment to meet again at exactly the same place and the same hour twenty years later. What is more touching about the story is the fact that through all these years they had never forgotten their promises and that they both kept the appointment—Bob had traveled a thousand miles only to meet his friend and thought it was worth it if his friend would turn up, and Jimmy's appearance on the spot at exactly the appointed hour was no accident either. We don't know what had happened twenty years before, which made them so special to each other. But it was clear that Jimmy was a good honest man and always true to his friend and Bob admired and trusted him. The drama of the story lies in the fact that when they met again twenty years later, they should find themselves on opposite sides of the law—one was the man wanted by the police and the other had turned out to be the police officer instructed to watch out for the runaway criminal. But no matter how much Jimmy cherished their friendship, he would not let a personal relationship stand in the way of performing his duty. On the other hand, although Jimmy had him arrested, Bob did not seem to be angry or bitter, for he knew clearly that since his best friend had become a police officer, he would not have acted otherwise. The fact that his hand shook when he read Jimmy's note towards the end of the story probably showed that he was saddened by the irony of the whole situation.

**2. Characterization**

Characterization: the method a writer uses to present to the reader the characters in a work of literature. Basically, writers use two ways—showing and telling. In showing they tell readers what the characters say and do; in telling, they may describe their characters' physical appearance, personality, even their thoughts and feelings.

In this story, the two main characters, Jimmy and Bob, are very well portrayed. Jimmy was probably of average height, a bit slow, but strongly built; not so adventurous, but honest, truthful, responsible and devoted. He was obviously a good police officer, and there was something about him that made him "a perfect picture of a guardian of the peace". These qualities are vividly and convincingly presented through the writer's description, the conversation between Jimmy and Bob and the way Jimmy actually handled the situation.

Bob is almost entirely presented through the method of showing. Careful readers can find many clues about Bob's life after he left New York and reach conclusions on the basis of such clues. He looked smart, restless, ambitious and ready to try in every way to reach his goal. He had made his fortune (wearing a watch set with diamonds), had broken the law (wanted by the police) and had many fights (the scar on his face). With some imagination, we can piece together his experiences in the past 20 year.

**3. Surprise ending**

O. Henry is known for surprise endings of his stories. This one is no exception. The story begins with a policeman on the beat. When the plainclothes man introduced himself as Jimmy we are taken in. We don't know which one of the two policemen was Jimmy until the very end of the story. When we read the story over again we find the ending is foreshadowed (=signs are provided for that ending).

#### 2 Structure

The text can be roughly divided into four parts, which can be further divided into several sections.

**Part I A policeman on the beat a few minutes before 10 p.m. (paras. 1–2)**

1. The policeman walked up the street impressively. (para. 1)
2. He looked like the guardian of peace on the beat. (para. 2)

**Part II The policeman's encounter with a man from the West (paras. 3–17)**

1. He walked up to a man standing in the doorway of a store. (para. 3)
2. The man explained to him he had come to keep an appointment. (paras. 4–6)
3. The man told the policeman about the appointment made 20 years before with his best friend Jimmy Wells. (paras. 7–9)
4. It seemed to the policeman the man from the West was rich. (paras. 10–13)
5. The policeman left a few minutes after ten o'clock. (paras. 14–17)

**Part III The arrest of the man from West by a plainclothes man (paras. 18–31)**

1. About 20 minutes later a man came and claimed he was Jimmy Wells. (paras. 18–21)
2. The two friends told each other about their life since they parted. (paras. 22–27)
3. Bob, the man from the West discovered the other man was not Jimmy Wells. (paras. 28–30)
4. The other man was a plainclothes man who came to arrest Bob, the criminal Chicago police wanted. (para. 31)

**Part IV A note from the best friend of the arrested man (paras. 32–33)**

1. The plainclothes man handed Bob a note from Jim Wells. (para. 32)
2. In the note, Jimmy explained why he didn't arrest Bob himself. (para. 33)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

**1. Dramatization**

The story lends itself to dramatization. It could be divided into three parts for this purpose: the scene at the "Big Joe" Brady's Restaurant, the encounter of the police officer with the man from the West, and Bob's arrest. Students are encouraged to use their imagination, and add whatever they think plausible to the story. They can even add a scene or two about the two friends' childhood and/or about Bob's life in prison. Teachers are advised to divide their class into groups of three for this activity, with one of them playing Bob, another Jimmy/the plainclothes man, and the third a narrator.

**2. Discussion**

1) Here are a few topics for oral practice.

■ Tell the story of Bob and that of Jimmy.

■ Make a character sketch of both Bob and Jimmy.

■ Imagine what the two men had been doing during the past 20 years.

2) Here are a few topics for discussion.

■ Do you think Jimmy did the right thing as a friend? If you were him, what would you do?

■ What do you think of Bob? Do you think a criminal can make a faithful friend? Do you think Bob is a plausible (seeming likely to be true, or able to be believed) character?

■ Do you think the friendship between Jimmy and Bob would survive after Bob's arrest? Give reasons to support your point of view.

■ Imagine how the story will continue after Bob's arrest.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

**1. The policeman on the beat... (para. 1)**

beat: an area of the town or city that a police officer regularly walks around

on/walking the beat: (of a policeman) on duty, walking around

**2. The impressiveness was normal and not for show, for spectators were few. (para. 1)**

The police officer walked that way habitually, not to attract attention or admiration because there were few people in the streets.

for show: with the purpose of looking attractive or impressive rather than being useful

**3. The time was barely ten o'clock, but chilly gusts of wind with a taste of rain in them had almost emptied the streets. (para. 1)**

barely: hardly, only just

More examples:

She spoke so softly. I could barely hear her.

I can barely make both ends meet. How can I buy a car?

a taste of rain: a small amount of rain

empty: to make (a place) empty, to cause people leave (a place)

More examples:

The TV series emptied the streets at 8 pm every Saturday.

He emptied his pockets to show that he really had no money on him.

The river finally empties into the Atlantic. (used as an intransitive verb)

**4. Trying doors as he went, swinging his club with many clever movements, turning now and then to cast his watchful eye down the peaceful street, the officer, with his strongly built form and slight air of superiority, made a fine picture of a guardian of the peace. (para. 2)**

This sentence conveys very important information about the police officer, he is described as the guardian of the peace on the beat.

cast one's eye on/over/down sth: to look at, or read sth quickly

eye: a particular way of seeing, judging or understanding sth

Questions to elicit responses from students:

1) What did the police officer do while walking the beat? What for? What does all this show about this police officer?

trying doors as he went: to find whether they were locked (his strong sense of responsibility) swinging his club with many clever movements: to warn off would-be troublemakers (his confidence and competence as a cop)

turning now and then to cast his watchful eye down the peaceful street: to notice possible danger or illegal activity (his vigilance as a policeman)

2) What made him look like a guardian of the peace?

his strongly built form: physically, he was strong enough to deal with lawbreakers (his strength)

slight air of superiority: spiritually, he was able to overpower criminals (his pride and sense of dignity as a law-enforcing officer)

**5. The area was one that kept early hours. (para. 2)**

People in that area closed their stores pretty early.

**6. all-night lunch counter (para. 2)**

all-night: open all night

Lunch counter: a counter in a store or restaurant where food is served or sold to be taken out

**7. Halfway down a certain block, the policeman suddenly slowed his walk. (para. 3)**

Question: Why did the policeman slow down?

Because he saw a man standing in the doorway and he became suspicious, or because he was excited that it might be the friend he had expected to see.

**8. In the doorway of a darkened hardware store a man leaned, with an unlighted cigar in his mouth. (para. 3)**

The writer was careful about the details in the description.

It had to be a darkened store and Bob's cigar had to be unlighted, otherwise the policeman would find that it was the man wanted by the police in Chicago.

**9. As the policeman walked up to him, the man spoke up quickly. (para. 3)**

walk up: to walk towards sb

speak up: to speak louder so that people can hear you

(Obviously, the man in the doorway didn't want the police officer to suspect him.)

**10. It's an appointment made twenty years ago. (para. 3)**

The man was eager to explain something to the police officer, which reflected the mentality of a man wanted by the police.

It is strange that they did not recognize each other. It could be the darkness and long separation. It could also be that Jimmy was cautious.

**11. Sounds a little funny to you, doesn't it? (para. 3)**

sound: a link verb here

More examples:

It may sound easy to you, but actually it is very difficult.

The name sounds familiar, but I can't recall where I heard it.

It sounds good. OK, let's do it.

**12. ... to make certain it's all straight (para. 4)**

... to make sure that it's all true. "Straight" here means "true, accurate", it can also mean

"honest, frank", as in the following examples

I'll be straight with you. You will lose this job if you don't work hard.

Yes or no? Can't you give me a straight answer?

**13. The light showed a pale, square-jawed face with keen eyes, and a little white scar near his eyebrow. (para. 6)**

The match light showed a face which must have fitted the description of the wanted man, especially the square jaw and the white scar.

keen eyes: sharp eyes

**14. His tiepin was a large diamond, oddly set. (para. 6)**

oddly set: the diamond placed in a strange or unusual way showing the owner's lack of taste. The mention of the diamond tiepin and that of the scar suggested what Bob had gone through in the West.

**15. Twenty years ago tonight... (para. 7)**

Similar expressions: ten years ago this morning/this time yesterday/at exactly this hour next Sunday

**16. The next morning I was to start for the West to make my fortune. (para. 7)**

Cultural note: In popular ideas about America, the Wild West has always meant the land of opportunities and adventures, where many European settlers moved during the 19th century to establish new farms and new cities. In films it is often shown as a place where cowboys and Native Americans fight each other, and where cowboys use guns rather than the law to settle arguments. Therefore, a situation where there are no laws or controls is sometimes described as being like the Wild West.

make one's fortune: to make a large amount of money

**17. ... no matter what our conditions might be... (para. 7)**

Notice this pattern:

no matter what = whatever     no matter when = whenever

no matter where = wherever     no matter who = whoever

no matter how = however

**18. We figured that in twenty years each of us ought to have our fate worked out and our fortunes made, whatever they were going to be. (para. 7)**

We thought by that time we would have found out our fate and known how much we have achieved materially—whether our fortune huge or small.

work out: to happen, develop, or end in a particular way or have a particular result

More examples:

The project didn't work out the way we wanted it to.

Things seem to be working out satisfactorily.

**19. Haven't you heard from your friend since you left? (para. 8)**

Negative general questions are often used to show surprise.

More examples:

Mary's back. Didn't you know?

Aren't you going? It's such a beautiful place.

Haven't you had supper yet? It's almost 10.

**20. "Well, yes, for a time we wrote," said the other. (para. 9)**

for a time: for a short period of time

The word "time" can form many useful phrases. Here are some of the most frequently used: ahead of time, all the time, at any time, at one time, at the same time, for the time being, from time to time, in time, in no time, on time

**21. ... we lost track of each other. (para. 9)**

... we lost contact with each other (neither of us knew where the other was).

keep/lose track of sb/sth: to pay attention to sb/sth so that you know where they are or what is happening to them, or fail to do this

More examples:

— Have you kept track of the stock market (股市)?

— I have lost track of its latest development.

The airport has lost track of the plane.

It is important to keep track of current affairs.

**22. You see, the West is a pretty big place, and I kept running around over it pretty lively. (para. 9)**

I kept moving around in the West, never staying in the same place for long. (That's why it was hard for us to keep track of each other.)

pretty lively: very quickly

**23. ... and it's worth it if my old partner turns up. (para. 9)**

be worth it: (informal) used to say that you gain something from an action

eg   The laptop is a bit expensive, but it was worth it.

turn up: to arrive at the place, especially in a way that is unexpected

More examples:

We waited for months before a good job opportunity turned up.

The police were ordered to arrest him as soon as he turned up.

**24. He was a kind of slow man, though, good fellow as he was. (para. 13)**

slow: not good and quick at understanding things

good fellow as he was: though he was a good fellow More examples:

Poor as they were, they were quite happy.

Famous as she was, she was very modest.

**25. I've had to compete with some of the sharpest brains going to get my money. (para. 13)**

In order to make money, I had to compete with the most shrewd and crafty people.

sharp brains: people who can understand and think quickly and are not easily deceived

**26. A man gets stuck in New York. It takes the West to make a man really keen. (para. 13)**

A man is unable to go very far or to be very successful in New York where life is boring. He has to go to the West to become an eager and exciting person.

The man from the West means that New York City was "civilized", it had too many laws and

that getting rich quickly was less likely. In the West, however, one could by-pass the rules, and through being tougher and smarter one could become rich very fast (though dishonestly).

**27. I'll be on my way. Hope your friend comes around all right. Are you going to leave immediately? (para. 15)**

I'll be on my way: I have to go now.

all right: without any problem

More examples:

I hope you can understand the book all right.

I can see all right.

Are you going to leave immediately: The police officer (Jimmy) wanted to make sure that Bob wouldn't escape while he was away

**28. "I should say not!" said the other. (para. 16)**

"Should" here is used to give emphasis to the statement.

**29. a fine, cold rain (para. 18)**

fine: thin

**30. The few foot passengers in that quarter hurried dismally and silently along with coat collars turned high and pocketed hands. (para. 18)**

foot passenger: people walking in the street

Today we use the word "pedestrian" to refer to a person walking in the street, and "foot passengers" usually means passengers on a boat, who are not in a car or other vehicles

in that quarter: in that part of the city

with coat collars turned high and pocketed hands: their collars were turned high and their hands were in theirs coat pockets

Question: Why had foot passengers turned up their collars?

They did it to keep themselves warm.

**31. to fill an appointment (para. 18)**

In modern English, "to keep an appointment" is more common. Another common collocation is "to make an appointment".

**32. ... with collar turned up to his ears... (para. 19)**

turned up: (the tall man's coat collar) was moved upward

The verb pattern is verb + adverb + object.

Question: Why had the man turned up the collar to his ears?

He did it to cover part of his face.

Note the phrase "turn up" in the following sentence: ... and it's worth it if my old partner turns up. (para. 9) Here "turn up" is a phrasal verb, meaning "to arrive" (refer to Note 23).

**33. "Bless my heart!" explained the new arrival... (para. 22)**

bless my heart: (old-fashioned, spoken) usually "bless my soul", used to express surprise

the new arrival: the man who has newly arrived

**34. It's Bob, sure as fate. (para. 22)**

sure as fate: (spoken) usually "(as) sure as hell", used for emphasis

**35. Moderately. I have a position in one of the city departments. (para. 26)**

moderately: reasonably, but not very well

I have a position: I have a job

in one of the city departments: (I have a job) in one of the departments of the city government

(He did not say straight that he had a position in the police department.)

**36. ... we'll go around to a place I know of, and have a good long talk about old times. (para. 26)**

know of sb/sth: (phrasal verb) to have heard of sb or sth and be able to give a small amount of information about them

have a good long talk about old times: have a hearty talk about those happy days we spent in the past.

**37. arm in arm (para. 27)**

臂挽臂

Draw students' attention to the adverbial use of these noun phrases

Similar expressions:

shoulder to shoulder 肩并肩, face to face 面对面, hand in hand 手拉手

**38. At the corner stood a drugstore, brilliant with electric lights. (para. 28)**

Note the inversion, in which the subject of the sentence "a drugstore" is placed after the main verb "stood" because the subject is a bit too long.

brilliant with electric lights: very bright because of its electric lights. "With" means "because of or caused by sb or sth".

More examples:

His face is red with anger.

Her fingers were numb with cold.

They jumped with joy.

They were trembling with fear.

**39. The man from the West stopped suddenly and released his arm. (para. 29)**

The man from the West suddenly stopped (talking about his life in the West) and withdrew his arm from the other man's elbow.

release: to stop holding sth

Other uses of "release":

The news was released in yesterday's New York Times. (to be made known)

She needed to do something to release her tension. (to get rid of)

We could release you from your duties for two days. Meanwhile, you take a good rest. (to allow you not to do your work)

**40. You've been under arrest for ten minutes, "Silky" Bob. (para. 31)**

"Under" here means "being in the state or the process of sth".

More examples:

under control, under heavy influence, under attack, under repair, under construction

"Silky" Bob: what Bob was known as in the underworld (≡the part of society consisting of criminal organizations and activities)

**41. Chicago... wants to have a chat with you. (para. 31)**

"Chicago" here refers to the Chicago police. "Wants to have a chat with you" is a euphemism (≡a word or phrase used to avoid saying an unpleasant or offensive word), meaning "is trying to track you down, arrest you and take you to court".

**42. Going quietly, are you? That's sensible. (para. 31)**

You are going quietly, will you? / You are not going to resist or put up a fight, are you?

That's the right thing to do for you.

**43. Now, before we go on to the station... (para. 31)**

What station did the tall man refer to?

The police station.

**44. ... I saw it was the face of the man wanted in Chicago. (para. 33)**

the man wanted: the man the police is trying to find and arrest

eg   He was wanted for the cruel murder of three children.

**45. Somehow I couldn't do it myself, so I went around and got a plain-clothes man to do the job. (para. 33)**

Somehow I couldn't arrest you myself, so I had a plain clothes man do it.

Jimmy had mixed feelings. He knew what his duty was. But the memories of their friendship, the expressions of Bob's undying respect and admiration for him and the fact that Bob had come all the way from a thousand miles away just to keep the appointment made 20 years before must have deeply touched him. Therefore, he could not bring himself to arrest Bob.

### III Translation of Text A

二十年后

欧·亨利

正在自己辖区巡逻的警察神气十足地沿街走了过来。他每次巡逻都是这样，他的这种神气绝不是为了引人注目，此时街上已经没有什么人了。现在不过刚刚晚上十点，但是夹杂着些许细雨的阵阵凉风几乎把街上的人都赶回家了。

他一边用各种巧妙的姿势挥动着警棍，一边检查各家各户的门是否锁好，不时转过身警惕地前后打量那条平静的街道，他体格健壮，神情略带威严，这一切使他俨然成了这里宁静生活的卫士。这里的人们习惯早睡早起，你有时会看到一家雪茄烟店或一个通宵营业的食品店还亮着灯，但绝大多数店铺早已打烊。

走到一个街区的中间，警察突然放慢了步伐，一个男人靠在一家熄了灯的五金店门口，嘴里叼着一支没有点燃的雪茄烟。警察走了过去，那人急忙解释起

## （续）

来。

“警官先生，我没事，”他坦然地，“我只是在这儿等一个朋友，这是个二十年前的约定。你觉得这有些不可思议，是不是？你要是想弄清楚我说的是不是实话，我就给你解释解释。在二十多年前，就在这儿，现在是五金店的那个地方，有一家叫‘大个子’乔·布雷迪的餐馆。”

“那餐馆五年前还在，”警察接着说，“后来给拆除了。”

店门口的男人划了一根火柴，点燃了那支雪茄。火光中显现出了他苍白的面庞，方正的下颌，机警的双眼，眉梢上有一小块白色伤疤。他的领带上别着一颗硕大的钻石，样子很怪。

“二十年前的今天晚上，”他说，“我和我最好的朋友吉米·韦尔斯在这家‘大个子’乔·布雷迪餐馆一块儿吃晚饭。吉米是我最好的朋友，也是世界上最好的人。我们一起在纽约长大，情同手足。那会儿，我十八，吉米二十。第二天早晨，我就要动身到西部去闯天运了。吉米不愿意离开纽约，谁也拉不走他，在他眼里世界上就纽约这一个地方。那天晚上我俩约定，二十年后的同一天，同一时间，不管我们那时混得怎么样，也不管我们离这儿有多远，我俩一定还在这家餐馆见面。我们估摸二十年后，不管我们真的混得怎么样，我们的人生也该有个模样了，钱也该攒下一些了。”

“嗯，听着倒挺有意思的，”警察说，“不过我觉得，你们两次见面相隔的时间真是不短啊。你走后就没有和这位朋友通信吗？”

“哦，有过，不过有那么一两次我们通信，”那人说，“可是一两年以后，我们失去了联系。要知道，西部很大，在那儿我老是东奔西跑。不过我相信要是吉米还活着的话，他肯定会来见我的。他一直是这世上最忠诚、最要好的朋友。他是决不会忘记这场约会的。今晚我千里迢迢赶到这里来，站在这个门口等他，只要我的老伙伴出现，那就值了。”

等朋友的人掏出一块漂亮的怀表，表盖上镶着一些小钻石。

“十点差三分，”他说，“当年我们是 10 点整在这家餐馆门口分手的。”

“看来你在西部干得不错嘛？”警察问道。

“说得对！但愿吉米干得有我一半那么好。虽说他是一个好人，他可不怎么机灵。我为了挣到我那些钱，可得跟那帮脑袋瓜贼精的人竞争。在纽约，人们很难有多大的作为，人只有到西部去才能有出息、干大事。”

警察挥舞着警棍，往前迈开了步子。

“我得走了。但愿你的朋友会来。你很快就会走吗？”

“肯定不会，”那人说，“我至少要再等他半小时，只要他还活在世上，到时他一定会来的。”

“再见，警官先生。”

“再见，先生，”警察继续在他的辖区巡逻。

这时下起了清冷的细雨，风刮个不停。街上仅有几个行人都把衣领翻得高高的，双手插在衣袋里，阴沉地、静悄悄地赶路。从千里之外赶来与年轻时的朋友相会的那个人仍然站在五金店门口，抽着雪茄，继续等待着。

他等了二十分钟后，一个身穿长大衣的高个子急匆匆地从街对面走了过来，他的大衣领子向上翻到耳朵处，他径直走到正在等人的男子的身边。

“是你吗，鲍勃？”来人迟疑地问道。

“是你吗，吉米·韦尔斯？”站在门口的男子叫了起来。

“天哪！”来人叫道，紧紧握住对方的双手。“没错，就是鲍勃。我早就知道要是你还活着，我肯定能在这儿见到你。哎呀，二十年的时间可不短啊，鲍勃。那家老餐馆已经不在了。要是那餐馆没拆那该多好，我们就能在那儿再吃顿饭了！老朋友，在那边过得怎么样？”

“得到了我想要的一切。吉米，你的变化也不小啊。我可没想到你会长这么高。”

“哦，二十岁以后我真是蹿高了点儿。”

“吉米，你在纽约干得还行吧？”

“还凑合，我在市政府的一个部门上班。鲍勃，我知道附近一个地方不错，我们去那儿好好叙叙旧吧。”

两人相互挽着对方的胳膊沿街走去。西边来客对自己的成就十分得意，开始简述他的发家史，另一个人兴致勃勃地听着，翻起的大衣领子几乎把他的脸全遮住了。

街角处有一家药店里面灯火明亮。这两人走到亮处时，不约而同地打量起了对方的脸。

西边来客突然止步，抽回了自己的胳膊。

“你不是吉米·韦尔斯，”他厉声说，“二十年不算短，不过改变不了一个人的鼻子的大小。”

“二十年却可以让一个好人变成坏人，”高个子说，“‘圆滑的’鲍勃，你已经被捕十分钟了。芝加哥警方猜到你可能到我们这里来，他们来电想和你谈谈。你是放聪明点，老老实实地跟我走吧。噢，在去警察局前，有人托我给你捎来一个便条，你可以先到药店的窗边先看看，这是韦尔斯警官给你的。”

西边来客接过递给他的小纸条读了起来。开始他尚平静，读完后拿着纸条的那只手微微地颤抖了一下。便条并不长，上面写道：

鲍勃：我准时到达了我们的约会地点，当你划亮火柴点雪茄时，我看清了你的脸，正是芝加哥警方所通缉的罪犯。可我说什么也下不了手，所以我走了，我找了一位便衣来替我做这件事。

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Lead-in

1. Two examples from Chinese literature where the characters deal with the situation in opposite ways:
> In *Water Margin*, Li Kui's brother immediately went to report to the local government when Li Kui secretly returned home to fetch his blind old mother so that he could take better care of her, because he knew Li Kui was a wanted outlaw.
> In *The Romance of Three Kingdoms*, Guan Yu, a general of Kingdom Shu, defied orders from his superior and let Cao Cao, the de facto ruler of the soon-to-be Kingdom Wei, through the Huarong Pass unharmed after Cao was defeated at the Battle of Chibi, because Cao had treated him very nicely while he was under Cao's service.

2. I have read a few of O. Henry's short stories and like them very much, such as *Gift of the Magi*, *The Cop and the Anthem*, etc. What makes his stories gripping is how he delves into the love, hate, happiness and predicament of the common people, how he talks about tragedy with a touch of humor and always excites tearful smiles in the reader, how he dramatizes turns of fate, twists of destiny, accidents of chance, and of course how he always winds up his stories with a surprising O. Henry ending.

#### Language Focus

**1**

adjectives/verbs: empty, close, slow, light
nouns/verbs: pocket, blow, beat

**2**

1. belong to, change into, compete with, hear from sb., know of, lose track of, speak up, start for, start up the street, tear down, turn up, walk up to sb., work out, be set with small diamonds
2. at least, for a time, for show, in existence, on earth, on one's way, on the beat, on time, under arrest, with the air of, by that time
3. a gust of wind, all right, arm in arm, sure as fate, now and then, so long, bless my heart

**3**

cast a watchful eye down the street, keep early hours, swing one's club, empty the streets, slow one's walk, make an appointment, fill the appointment, make a picture of, make one's fortune, strike a match, release one's arm, unfold the paper, light a cigar, do the job, outline the history

**4**

1. ... we agreed... that we would meet here again exactly twenty years from that date and time, no matter what our conditions might be... (para. 7)
Clauses introduced by "no matter what/who/which/when/where/how..."
2. We figured that in twenty years each of us ought to have our fate worked out and our fortunes made, whatever they were going to be. (para. 7)
Clauses introduced by "whatever" and the use of "have"
have sth. done: have our fate worked out
3. He was a kind of slow man, though, good fellow as he was. (para. 13)
Inverted "as" clauses indicating concession
4. Trying doors as he went... (para. 2)
as/while 解
5. Twenty years is a long time, but not long enough to change the size of a man's nose. (para. 30)
Infinitive phrase used as adverbial of result
6. It sounds pretty interesting. (para. 8)
The use of some action verbs turned into link verbs. More examples: appear, look, taste, feel, go, come, grow, prove, turn, stay, remain, get, etc.

7. About that long ago there used to be a restaurant where this store stands... (para. 4)
Use of "there..." More examples: There seems to be.../There ought to be.../There may have been.../There are said to be.../There are supposed to be..., etc.

#### Reading Comprehension

**1**

This is a story about two (1) young American friends from New York, Bob and Jimmy Wells (2) grew up together when they were young boys, but then the time came when they had to part. Bob wanted (3) to go out West (to make his fortune), while Jimmy Wells decided to (4) stay in New York. The two friends hated to say goodbye, so when the time came to say farewell, they dined at a restaurant and promised that they would (5) meet again there at the same time twenty years later, no matter what their conditions might be or from what distance they might have to come. The dramatic irony of the story is the fact that when the two friends finally met for their reunion twenty years later, they found themselves to be on the opposite sides of (6) the law/the road. Bob proved to be the man wanted by the (7) Chicago police, and Jimmy Wells was a New York policeman. They met at the appointed time and place, but Bob did not (8) recognize Jimmy, partly because it was dark, and they had not seen each other for twenty years, but more importantly perhaps because Bob had never expected his best friend would be (9) the police officer there to arrest him. Jimmy could not bring himself to do the job, so he found an excuse and went off to ask (10) a plain-clothes man to do it for him, with a note for Bob telling him that he was the policeman he had met a moment ago, at the exact time and place to fulfill their promised appointment.

**2**

1. The police officer on duty in this area walked toward north of the avenue in a way that would impress people, but he walked that way out of habit, not to attract people's admiration, because there were few people in the streets.
2. The policeman tried doors now and then to make sure they were properly locked while he walked along the streets, moving his club back and forth in clever ways, and turned often to look down the street to make sure that everything was in order. The policeman was strong and proud of being a law-enforcement officer, which made him a perfect picture of a protector of the peace.
3. A man is unable to go very far or to be very successful in New York where there are so many people and not enough chances for everybody. One has to go to the West to become eager and willing to compete and struggle.
4. This is a part of New York where businesses end early in the day without much night life. Besides, there was a fine rain falling and a steady cold wind blowing. So there were few foot passengers on the street. The few people visible were walking hurriedly and silently with their collars turned up and their hands deep in their pockets.
5. You are not going to resist or put up a fight, aren't you? That's the right thing to do for you.

**3**

1. The story begins with a description of the policeman. The policeman was Jimmy Wells, one of the two friends. The author didn't tell the readers because this creates suspense and also foreshadows the ending. The policeman made a fine picture of a guardian of the peace because of what he did: "trying doors as he went" to find whether they were locked (his strong sense of responsibility), "swinging his club with many clever movements" to warn off would-be troublemakers (his confidence and competence as a cop), "turning now and then to cast his watchful eye down the peaceful street" to notice possible danger or illegal activity (his vigilance as a policeman). Besides, "his strongly built form" indicates that physically, he was strong enough to deal with lawbreakers (his strength), and his "slight air of superiority" suggests that spiritually, he was able to overpower criminals (his pride and sense of dignity as a law-enforcing officer). The description is important in foreshadowing the development of the story, because this explains Jimmy's choice.

The setting is a dark, empty street at about ten o'clock in the evening with chilly gusts of wind. It is important because first of all it makes it reasonable for Bob to hide in the doorway of a darkened hardware store, and it creates a chilly, gloomy, suspenseful atmosphere.

2. I realize this policeman was Jimmy Wells only at the very end. He was walking his beat, but also meeting his friend to fulfill the appointment made twenty years ago. He didn't tell the man who he was because he realized he was the man wanted by Chicago police. He was sure it must be his friend Bob because Bob told him every detail about their appointment and their friendship, and Bob was there, at the appointed place at the appointed time.

3. Bob must have seen the policeman walking up to him for some time when he was walking the beat, as he was standing in the dark. He spoke up "quickly" but "confidently" because he didn't want the policeman to suspect him. He didn't recognize Jimmy, partly because he was too eager to impress the policeman of his innocence, partly because it was rather dark, and maybe also because he never thought Jimmy would make a policeman. He told the policeman that he was waiting for a friend, with whom he made an appointment twenty years ago when they parted to make their separate fortunes that they should meet there again exactly twenty years from that date and time.

4. Bob certainly thought he had realized his dream of making a fortune. He must have spent his twenty years engaging in some illegal ways of fortune-making in the West, probably with some fierce competition and violence involved; he certainly had made a lot of money. Jimmy had spent his twenty years in New York being an honest and responsible policeman.

5. She is Chicago; "having a chat" is a euphemism for having him arrested and interrogating him about the crimes he has committed. I guess Bob was known as "Silly Bob" because he was cunning and he managed to slip through the fingers of the police many times.

6. Jimmy wrote a note to tell Bob that he had come to fulfill the appointment and that he couldn't stand to arrest his old friend by himself. He couldn't do the job himself because it would be a betrayal to their friendship. Bob must have felt betrayed at first, but happy that his friend had also come for the appointment. The tremble of his hand suggested the complexity of his feelings: a sense of being betrayed, the happiness of having a friend who was loyal to their friendship, maybe a little anger at Jimmy for not revealing himself, and a little regret for what he himself had done.

7. The ending surprised me in that the policeman, who seemed an innocent passer-by, turned out to be Jimmy, and that every detail makes sense when I think back after reading the ending. It is natural for the two friends to have led such different lives, because they had very different aspirations and had taken divergent ways to achieve such aspirations; also, it could be seen that they had different personalities, Bob being more audacious and adventurous, while Jimmy being more peaceful and responsible.

8. I like the suspense, the surprise ending, the particularly apt descriptions, the elaborate arrangement of details that foreshadow the development, as well as the conflict between loyalty to a friend and one's public responsibility and moral principles. Examples of such stories can be found in the answer to the lead-in questions. Jimmy must have felt torn, and he chose a way that somewhat satisfied both his loyalty and his public responsibility. If I were Jimmy, I would tell Bob face-to-face who I was and tried to persuade him to turn himself in.

#### Language Exercises

##### 1 Expand your vocabulary

1.
1) weakness 2) quickness 3) strength 4) width 5) breadth 6) heat 7) anger 8) clarity 9) length 10) silence 11) freedom 12) sadness 13) moderation 14) happiness
A summary of noun-forming suffixes: -ness, -th, -ity, -ence, -dom, -ion

2.
Those that can be used as verbs are:
slow, clear, narrow, open, quiet, dim, thin, clean, empty, close, dry, wet, cool, near, dirty, blind
The corresponding verbs for the other adjectives are:
1) Many of these adjectives form their corresponding verbs by adding the verb suffix "-en" to them:
short→shorten; dark→darken; bright→brighten; sad→sadden; red→redden; ripe→ripen; weak→weaken; wide→widen; thick→thicken; quick→quicken; hard→harden; deep→deepen; broad→broaden; fat→fatten; light→lighten; loose→loosen; deaf→deafen
2) Some corresponding verbs of adjectives are formed by adding the verb suffixes "-en" to their corresponding nouns:
strong→strengthen; long→lengthen; high→heighten
3) Special cases:
hot→heat; cold→cool; low→lower; angry→anger

Note:
rich→enrich, large→enlarge (verbs are formed by adding prefix "en-"); "loose" can also be used as a verb, though considered formal, "long" when used as a verb, the meaning is different from the opposite of "short," 意为"盼望"; "fine," "low," and "wrong" can be verbs too, but they are converted from nouns and belong to a different category (The meanings of "fine," "low," and "wrong" are different too. "fine" means "罚款"; "low" means "牛叫的声音"; "wrong" means "冤枉"). Also notice that the verb "fasten" comes from the adjective "fast," meaning "紧挨的，贴在上面的"; "light" can also be used as a verb, meaning "点火".

**2**

1. 显然，他认为自己是个国家很有前途的政治人物。

#### Unit Project

1. Tips for students:
This is an introductory speech, so collecting and choosing information is essential. The speech could be composed of:
> A very brief introduction of Pu and his status in Chinese literature
> A very brief summary of the features of his short stories
> Introduction to one story that impresses you most and how it represents the features
> Your own comments on Pu and his stories

2. Tips for students:
Students are encouraged to be creative on this part. Try to imitate O. Henry's writing style.
Note: The addition of details and sequel will not necessarily make a short story more interesting and definitely will make it less subtle and suspenseful.
