#### Unit Project

##### 1.

It's true that certain animals are habitually described as good characters and certain other animals as bad characters, which is true in most cultures. For example, foxes are usually evil characters while dogs are usually good characters in Chinese culture. It is not justifiable and leads to stereotyped views in different cultures.

##### 2. Sample essay:

Many people like to keep animals as pets. Some just have one pet while other people keep many different pets. This often depends on if they live in a big or small house.
For a person who lives in a small house, a small pet is preferred. They could keep a bird or two as these are very interesting pets. They have brightly colored feathers and make nice chirping sounds. If you have a parrot, it can even learn to talk. But it takes a long time to teach it to say some words. Fish are also a type of pet for people without much space or time. They just need some food and their bowl or tank cleaning sometimes.
People that have more space and time to look after a pet could keep something bigger that needs more care, such as a dog or cat. Cats do not need to be looked after as much as a dog, but they get lonely if there is no one there with them. They often like to have someone to sit with and who strokes them. This makes them happy and they start to purr. If someone has a dog, they need to take it out for walks so that it can get exercise. It is good to take a dog out twice a day. Dogs need exercise so they cannot be kept in the house all the time. There are pets that need a very large amount of space such as horses or donkeys, and even domestic pigs. For these pets, a person needs more than a big garden but a field for them to move, run around and eat grass.

---

# Unit 9

## Text A · The Greatest Invention · Lord Dunsany

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

On the surface, this text is just a funny little story, not to be taken seriously. And one may also frown on the way the author seemed to be poking fun at some Caribbean countries where some dictators dreamed of conquering the world. But if we read more carefully, we find that the story does contain quite a few important things for us to think about. In other words, it should be read as a modern fable.

The first thing that occurs to our mind is the realistic description of the world situation today. In view of the latest scientific and technological developments, many dangerous weapons of mass destruction appeared: nuclear, biochemical, and electronic. For the first time in human history, it is possible for a few individuals to hold the whole humanity hostage. Since the September 11 incident, everybody has been talking about the threat of terrorism to human civilization. It will not be easy to find a solution. But now is the time to start thinking. It may very well be our last chance.

The next question naturally will be the purpose of science. We all agree that science is knowledge and knowledge is power. But power for what? Different people have different purposes in mind. There are people who want science to help them make more profits. There are people who want it to enable them to destroy, kill and conquer. It is obvious that science is not an absolute good. It is a double-edged sword. It can bring happiness to us. But it can also turn out to be Pandora's Box. This will lead to the next question, that is, what should scientists do in the circumstances? Should they just concentrate on their research without bothering about the social consequences of their discoveries or should they give their attention to their moral responsibility for what they bring to humanity?

Another interesting question has to do with the driving force of scientific development. It is often said that necessity is the mother of invention. Should science always aim at serving a practical need? Especially in modern society, scientific research is becoming an increasingly time- and money-consuming undertaking. Therefore it is unrealistic to develop science without the support of business and government. However, there are also people who believe that the greatest motivating power for scientific research lies in human instinct: the desire to know, or the idle intellectual curiosity. In fact, they might argue that government and business tend to create an obstacle for scientific development.

These people might compare Carasierra with Archimedes when the latter jumped out of the bathtub and shouted "I've found it! I've found it!"

#### 2 Structure

The text can be roughly divided into three parts, which can be further divided into several sections.

**Part I The introduction: Jorkens was asked what he thought the future of the world would be like. In answering the question, he recalled a story.** (paras. 1–4)

**Part II The body: Jorkens retold the story he heard from a man who used to work for the Ministry of Warfare of a small Caribbean country.** (paras. 5–44)

1. Jorkens described how he met the man and made him tell the story. (para. 5)
2. The story the man told Jorkens (paras. 6–44)
   1) The military ambition of his country: domination of the whole Caribbean
   2) Their attempt to realize the ambition
      a. They got rid of their Minister of Warfare.
      b. They adopted modern warfare, depending on the intelligence/wisdom of scientists.
      c. They tried to make the brilliant scientist, Carasierra, invent a deadly germ.
      d. They had Carasierra executed when he turned his mind to inventing a living blade of grass.

**Part III The conclusion of Jorkens' story: The man with a mustache fell silent as if still thinking about what his country almost had achieved.** (para. 45)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1) Was Carasierra a great scientist from the point of view of the man Jorkens talked to? Why or why not? Have students discuss what makes a great scientist
2) Carasierra declared that he did not work for use. Let students discuss if they think this a right attitude for scientists
3) Encourage students to discuss how we can make our country one of the leading countries in scientific development

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

1. **"What do you think is going to happen, Jorkens?" one of us asked one day at the club.** (para. 1)
Notice that the story about the scientist was first told in the first person by the man with a big mustache Jorkens met in a Caribbean country. It was then retold by Jorkens to us one day at the club. Jorkens was obviously a member of our club who was probably generally acknowledged as the most well-informed and knowledgeable. Therefore we asked him that day what he thought was going to happen in the world.

2. **"There are so many inventions... of which we know nothing."** (para. 4)
Draw the students' attention to the grammar exercises on "of which", "about which", "on which", "for which", "against which"..., etc.

3. **"Now that a man can carry in a bag a bomb that is more powerful than several battleships, it is hard to find out what any country can do or will do next."** (para. 4)
This is no longer science fiction. When the technology for producing these terrible weapons is so easily available, the threat to the safety of the people in the world is only too real. Now the question is what we should do about this.

4. **I was on a ship in the tropics, and we put into a port.** (para. 5)
**in the tropics:** in the tropical area. Judging by the context, the story must have been set in the tropical areas near the Caribbean Sea.
**put into/in a port:** to enter a port (said of a ship)

5. **I was tired of looking at the tropical sea, so I went ashore and walked into a tavern to see if they had any decent wines in that country.** (para. 5)
**go ashore:** to go to the shore. "Ashore" here is an adverb.
Observe the following adverbs containing the prefix "a-":
They were walking abreast.
He was standing a few steps apart from them.
She left the door ajar.
Many of those visitors came from afar.
**decent:** acceptable, good enough
eg a decent meal, decent clothes, a decent job, a decent house, decent wages, a decent hotel, etc

6. **... with... a certain look in his eyes that made me wonder if he might not have something interesting to tell.** (para. 5)
Somehow the way the man looked made Jorkens feel that this man probably had something interesting to tell. But the story did not say why.

7. **... I called for a bottle of the strange local wine.** (para. 5)
I ordered/asked for a bottle of the strange local wine.

8. **When the bottle had been uncorked and the wine poured out, like liquid tropical sunlight, I watched it go down under that black mustache.** (para. 5)
**and the wine poured out:** and the wine had been poured out ("Had been" is omitted.)
Refresh the students' memory of the use of the prefix "un-" in verbs.
eg unlock, unzip, undress, unwrap, unfold, uncap, uncover, unload
**like liquid tropical sunlight:** It suggests that the wine was pretty strong, like hot liquid sunlight.

9. **And when a certain amount had gone down, he began to talk.** (para. 5)
And when the man had downed a few glasses, when he was half drunk, and his tongue was considerably loosened, he began to talk.
Notice the way Jorkens described this man. He appeared quite ridiculous, quite a drunkard really, but later on we learned that he had been a government official. The author probably just wanted to add a few humorous touches, but they also showed a racist attitude towards Latin American people.

10. **"We aimed at the mastery of the whole Caribbean."** (para. 6)
"It was our aim or purpose to have complete control of the whole Caribbean."
Notice the different meanings of the word "mastery" in this sentence:
Everybody was impressed by her quick mastery of those complicated theories.

11. **"War is no longer a matter of armies; it depends on the intelligence of scientists."** (para. 6)
Modern wars do not depend on how big an army you have. The number of troops are no longer decisive. You depend more and more on high-tech, which means you depend more and more on the intelligence of scientists. We should no doubt notice this new trend. But this does not mean that we can now come to the conclusion that whoever has the most advanced science and technology is invincible and therefore can dictate to the whole world what they should or should not do.

12. **"And we had a scientist who, as I have since seen proved, had no rival west of the Atlantic."** (para. 6)
**as I have since seen proved:** as I have seen him proved without rival
**have no rival:** to be the very best
**west of the Atlantic:** referring to Latin American countries

13. **"You shall hear."** (para. 8)
"Shall" here is used to show a promise, meaning that something is definite, certain to happen.
This use is formal and old-fashioned.

14. **... he was not at all what one would regard as the figure of a soldier.** (para. 11)
**the figure of a soldier:** the kind of person one would expect to see as a soldier, tall and strong
Notice that the word "figure" is usually used to refer to the shape of a woman's body.
eg She has a wonderful figure.
You have to watch your diet if you want to keep your figure.
The word "figure" has many other meanings as it: the exact figure (number), an important figure (person), a five-digit figure (money), to play the figure of a judge (character)
This sentence could be re-rendered as:
... he was not at all the kind of person one would regard as a soldier.
Ask students to make more sentences with: what we would regard as / what we might call / what one might describe as / what you would consider, etc.

15. **"Our Minister... was a cavalry officer and could not adapt his ideas to modern science. He thought of war simply as an opportunity for cavalry charges and fine uniforms and glory ..."** (para. 12)
**thought of war as:** regarded war as
**cavalry charges:** charges on horseback, charge: a rush to the enemy to attack
**fine uniforms and glory:** beautiful uniforms and impressive appearance
**adapt to:** This is not a new word. Just give the students a few more sentences to translate to enhance their memory:
eg You have to adapt your teaching method to the kind of students you are dealing with now.
He found it difficult to adapt to the new conditions.

16. **"We had to get rid of him in order to fulfill our just aspirations."** (para. 12)
**get rid of him:** to get him out of the way or to remove him from office. Under a dictatorship it often means physical liquidation, involving a coup d'état (政变).
**fulfill our just aspirations:** to achieve our just goals
**aspiration:** a strong desire to have or achieve sth
Observe the following common collocations of the verb "fulfill": fulfill a hope/a promise/a wish/a dream/a pledge/one's duty/one's potential

17. **"And it is just that we should have it."** (para. 14)
Make sure that the students do not mix up this "just" with the adverb "just".

18. **"Once, if a nation had twelve battleships it was a Great Power..."** (para. 16)
It refers to the 19th century when colonialists and imperialists pursued gunboat diplomacy.
"Once" here means "at one time" or "there used to be a time" or "in the past". It should not be mixed up with the conjunction "once".
eg Once you leave the country, you'll miss a lot of things.
Once you have power, you are also likely to abuse it.
Once you are seriously ill, you'll know how important it is to be healthy.
"Once" in these sentences means "the moment (something happens)".

19. **But what if we know how to let loose a plague capable of destroying whole nations?"** (para. 16)
"What if" is a shortening of "What would happen if".
*More examples:*
What if the equipment doesn't arrive in time?
What if Jane refuses to play the wicked step-mother?
Simple as it may seem, this sentence actually is quite complicated grammatically. Ask the students to analyze the structure.
**let loose:** to let sb or sth go free
**plague:** any diseases causing death and spreading quickly to a large number of people.
Here, it specifically refers to the germ warfare, a deliberate war act of spreading germs in order to kill as many people as possible, combatants and civilians alike.

20. **"We looked for a new and deadlier germ."** (para. 18)
**deadly:** 1) (adj) very dangerous, likely to cause death
eg deadly poison, deadly enemy (bitter enemy), deadly silence (complete silence)
2) (adv) deadly serious (very serious), deadly boring (very boring)

21. **"We knew that we had marvelous powers within our grasp, if only Carasierra could be kept at his work."** (para. 18)
**have sth within one's grasp:** to be able to achieve or attain sth
eg They were quite sure that they had the championship within their grasp.
Humanity already has the technology within their grasp to send the first man to the Mars.
**if only:** Here it is used to introduce a conditional clause to express what you think should at least be done to achieve a purpose.
*More examples:*
I would travel round the world, if only I were twenty years younger.
We could have a much better harvest if only we had more water.
"If only" is usually used to express a strong wish.
*More examples:*
If only I knew there would be a terrible earthquake!
If only my mother were still alive!
**could be kept at his work:** the passive form of "could keep sb at his work"
**keep sb at sth:** to force sb to continue to do sth
This expression can also be used in the following sentence:
To do exercise once in a while is not enough. You've got to keep at it.

22. **"Idle, was he?" I said, for I thought it likely in a hot country like that.** (para. 19)
"Idle, was he?" = "He was idle, was he not?"
*More examples:*
Now satisfied, are you?
Sick again, is she?
**idle:** inactive; lazy

23. **"Always spurred on by a fierce ambition. His very life was devoted to making inventions."** (para. 20)
**always spurred on by a fierce ambition:** always driven by a fierce ambition
**fierce:** very strong, done with very strong feelings and energy
*Also:* fierce emotions, fierce attack, fierce competition
**very:** (adj) used to emphasize a noun
eg He died that very day.
This was the very place I found it.
**devote one's life to (doing) sth:** Other nouns that can be used here are: time, energy, money, effort, etc.

24. **"... but we forgot his folly."** (para. 20)
**folly:** a very stupid thing to do, especially sth that will have serious consequences to the person who does it
It is interesting how scientists are often misunderstood. We know in history that even the greatest scientists have been regarded as foolish, if not dangerous, at one time. No wonder Emerson would say "To be great is to be misunderstood".

25. **"... he was more than content."** (para. 23)
**content:** (adj) happy and satisfied
This word is not used before nouns. We can say, "He was quite content." But we have to say, "He died with a contented look."

26. **"I tried everything: threats, appeals to him to think of our ancient glory, even bribes."** (para. 23)
**think of our ancient glory:** to think of his patriotic duty to help to recover our past glory
**bribe:** 1) (n) sth, especially money, offered or given in bribing
2) (v) to pay sb as a bribe
The word "bribe" is used in the following patterns:
He tried to bribe the custom officers to let them smuggle in cars.
He was able to bribe the head of the personnel department for his promotion.
She refused to accept the bribes (bribery).

27. **"But nothing would turn him from his project. The splendor of his new inspiration gripped him, and he was like a man drugged."** (para. 23)
**turn him from his project:** to divert his attention from his project, to make him give up that project
**project:** an important and carefully planned piece of work such as education projects, construction projects, research projects, etc
**splendor:** impressive beauty
**gripped him:** held his attention and interest very powerfully
**he was like a man drugged:** He was like a man who had been drugged.

28. **"And the splendor of our position faded like dreams."** (para. 24)
**position:** Here, it refers to the places the man's country was in in relation to other countries.
**fade (away):** to gradually disappear (color, hopes, memory, dream, beauty, etc)

29. **We were so nearly one of the Great Powers but for a fancy that came to this man's mind.** (para. 24)
**but for a fancy:** if not for a fancy; if a fancy had not gripped this man

30. **"But there was a queer light in his eyes that day, and really I think he hardly heard me. He would only say, 'I have done it, have done it.'"** (para. 26)
The way the man described the scientist was amusing. He obviously could not understand why the scientist behaved the way he did. He thought that he was stupid and queer, like a man drugged, because he could not make him afraid, and could not lure him with money. The scientist was completely wrapped in his own thoughts, and was intoxicated in his new discovery. In face of this true scientific spirit, the man must have appeared totally helpless. Notice that the word "queer" nowadays is often used to refer to homosexuals, therefore avoid using this word unless that is what you mean.

31. **"I saw only a square yard of grass, marked off with a strip of white tape."** (para. 31)
This scientist obviously was more interested in creating rather than destroying life.
**mark an area off:** to make an area separate by drawing a line around it or putting a rope around it (Here: by putting a strip of white tape around it)
**a strip of:** a thin narrow piece of (paper, cloth, tape, sand)
cf a slip of paper, a streak of white hair, a stretch of water, a strand of wire

32. **"You see no blade different from the rest?"** (para. 34)
This is what some grammarians call "an intonation question", a statement in terms of the word order, but a question in meaning. It is used when the expected response is yes. Notice that this kind of questions always requires the use of the rising tone.

33. **"Then you have seen the most wonderful invention of all that man has made," he replied with a wild look in his eyes.** (para. 36)
**a wild look:** a crazy look, the way a person might look when he feels a strong and uncontrollable joy

34. **"I do not work for use, but for wonder."** (para. 38)
Ask the students to comment on this. Should we work for use or wonder? Is it possible for people to do scientific research simply for truth? Isn't the desire for money, position, recognition and other rewards absolutely necessary for successful work in the scientific field?

35. **"... and he gave me every facility, showing me the entire process in his laboratory."** (para. 44)
**gave me every facility:** gave me everything I needed to learn about the whole process

36. **"Whether it was that the stranger's tale was told," Jorkens concluded, putting down his glass, "or that the influence of the wine was over, he fell then to silent brooding, gazing, as it seemed, into the past at the grip that his country had lost on the Caribbean, perhaps on the world."** (para. 45)
Jorkens implied that the man told the story under the influence of the wine, that is, when he was drunk.
**concluded:** ended his narration
**brood:** to keep thinking for a long time about sth that makes you very angry or worried
**lose the grip on:** to lose the power and control over

## （续）

### IV Translation of Text A

最伟大的发明

邓萨尼勋爵

一天，在俱乐部里，我们当中有一个人问道：“乔肯斯，你觉得今后世界会发生什么事？”

乔肯斯说：“会发生什么事？那很难说。过去人们大概还能知道其他国家想干什么和能干什么，可是现在情况完全不一样了。”

“怎么不一样了？”那个人问道。

乔肯斯说：“现在有那么多发明我们都一无所知。今天单一个人都可以随身携带一把比几艘战列舰威力还要大的炸弹，所以就更难讲任何一个国家能做什么或下一步打算做什么了。我给你举个例子。”

当时我乘坐的船在热带地区航行（乔肯斯讲述道），后来停靠在一个港口。我当时已看腻了热带海洋，于是我就上了岸，走进一家小酒馆，想看看那个国家有什么像样的酒没有。结果还真没有。但那儿有几个蓄着小黑胡子的人，他的那种眼神让我感觉好像这人会有点什么有趣的事讲。于是我就问他，我能否请他喝一杯，他很爽快地接受了，于是我要了一瓶当地产的那种奇怪的酒，拔出瓶塞，倒出来的烈酒就像流动的热带阳光。我看着那酒流进黑胡子的嘴里，几杯下肚后，他就开始讲起来了。

“我们的目标是征服整个加勒比海地区，”他说。“你不要以为我们是个小国就不会成功。现在战争不再取决于军队；而是依靠科学家的智慧。而我们就曾有过一位科学家，迄今为止已证明他的智慧在整个大西洋以西的地区都无人能比。”

“你们证实了这点？”我忍不住问。

“是的，”他说。“你听我给你讲讲这个故事。”

我又要了一瓶酒放在他面前，我于是真的听到了这故事。

“你根本想不到吧，”他说，“我曾经在国防部工作过。”

我还真没想到，因为他看上去一点都不像当兵的。不过正如他解释给我听的，现在的战争已经完全变了。

他说：“我们的部长是骑兵军官出身，他的思想不能适应现代科学。他认为战争就是让骑兵有机会去冲锋陷阵，穿着漂亮的军装，去获得胜利的荣耀。所以，为了实现我们正义的雄心壮志，我们不得不把他除掉。”

“什么雄心壮志？”我问。

“就是控制整个加勒比地区啊，”他说。“我们这么做是完全有理由的。我们的人民就生在这个地方。”“当然了，”我用安慰他的口气说道，尽管我不知道他是在代表哪个国家说话。

“在那位国防部长被除掉以后，”他接着说，“我们就集中全力考虑现代战争，而且开始取得巨大的进步。现代战争给了小国家巨大的机会。以前，如果一个国家有十二艘战舰，那它就是个强国了。我们只能服从。但是，一旦我们知道如何放出一场可以完全毁掉其他国家的瘟疫，情况又会怎么样呢？难道我们还需要对我们合理的愿望保持沉默吗？不，我们要说出来。”

“当然，”我说。

“其他国家对细菌战也有些了解，”这个陌生人说，“不过我们寻求的是一种更新更致命的细菌，而且我们有这样一个人。他不仅能给我们制造出这种细菌，而且还能提供一种更有效的方法来散布这种细菌——这人的名字就叫西尔韦雷·卡拉西拉。我们知道我们手中拥有巨大的威力，只要我们能对卡拉西拉专心进行他的工作。”

“他有些懒，对吗？”我问，心中想在这样的炎热的国家里，人是很容易发懒的。

“不，”这个陌生人说，“他从来都不懒。他总是被一个强烈的愿望激励着。他一生都在揭发明创造。是的，他努力工作，努力为我们创造一种神奇的东西。噢，我们相信这个人的智慧，这样做是对的，但我们都忘了他的荒废。”

这个人沉默了片刻。

“卡拉西拉干什么了？”我问。

“他那个发明创造的愿望一直驱使着他，”他说。“他知道自己是世界上最伟大的科学家，而且决心向人们展示他的才华。只要他当时正研究的细菌看起来是所有发明中最伟大的，他就会心满意足。可是在他还没有完成这项研究的时候，另一个灵感进入了他的脑海，使他又去研究别的了。我们用尽了一切手段：威胁他，恳求他想想我们古代的辉煌，甚至贿赂他，但是什么手段也不能使他放弃自己的研究课题。他被自己这一新灵感的光芒深深迷住了，就像一个吸了毒的人一样无法自拔。”

“与此同时，我们即将称霸加勒比的美好地位也像梦一样消逝了。要不是一个荒诞的想法进入这个人的脑子里，我们差一点就成了一个伟大的强国。”

“卡拉西拉的荒诞想法究竟是什么？”我问。

“听我慢慢告诉你，”他说。“日复一日，我去他的实验室，恳求他，差不多含着眼泪恳求他回到为我们做研究的工作上去。但是没有用，他就是不听。我给了他所有的机会，但是我最后不得不以死来威胁他。我告诉他，要是他再不回去干他的正事，他就得被枪毙。但是那天他两眼放光，流露出一种奇怪的神。真的，我想他当时可能都没听到我在说什么。他只是不停地说：‘我做了，做成了！’”

“做成什么了？”我问他。“陌生人接着说。

“最伟大的发明，”他说，“人类迄今为止最最伟大的发明。”

“如果你再不干你该干的话，”我重复道，“你要被枪毙的。”

“这个工作更神奇，”他说。”

“好吧，给我看看，”我命令道。于是他带我出了实验室，来到他的草地上。然后他用手指一指，我只看到一块不到一平方米的用白色胶带划出的草坪。“这是什么？”我问。

“他把白胶带拿了起来，又划出了更小的一片，这次只有几平方英寸。‘你看没看到什么神奇的东西？’他问。‘仔细看看。’”

“我仔细细细看了看，说，‘我什么也没看见。’”

“‘那就是最神奇的地方，’他说，‘你有没有看到有哪片叶子与别的叶子不一样？’”

“‘没有，’我又说。”

“那你就见到了人类所有发明中最神奇的发明，‘他答道，两眼闪着兴奋的光芒，’因为其中有一片草是我自己创造的。‘”

“可那又有什么用？’我问。

“有用！有用！‘他重复着我用的字眼，笑道，’我工作不是为了有用，而是为了创造奇迹。‘”

“当我们统治加勒比的时候，‘我说，’那就是奇迹。‘”

“但是创造一片草叶比那个要神奇得多，‘他说。”

“这一点我敢承认，‘但我加了一句，’现在你该回到你原来的工作岗位上去了。‘”

“听到这句话，他更大声狂笑起来。”

“不，既然现在我可以创造出草叶，‘他喊道，’下一步我要去创造花朵了。‘”

“我仔细察看了他那片草叶。他还给我看了所有他放在实验室里制造这片草叶的整个过程。那片草叶十分完美，而且明显是活的。但他使我确信它确实是人工制造的。真是一个了不起的人。但是真遗憾，我们这些负责的部长们是不能作出威胁以后又说话不算数的。我已经用死威胁过他，所以他必须被处决。……’”

“不知是这个陌生人的故事讲完了呢，”乔肯斯放下酒杯，最后说，“还是那种奇怪的酒的作用消失了。他陷入了沉思，似乎在回想着过去，回想他的国家失去的统治加勒比海——或许是统治整个世界的机会。”

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Leading

1. To name a few, the internet, computer, television, telephone, car, light bulb, electricity, antibiotics, firearm and principle of relativity. Students' answers and reasons may vary. There will be no consensus on this question.

2. Factors that make inventions and discoveries possible: pure luck, sheer accident, knowledge stored, support of business and government and need of the market, etc.

#### Language Focus

1.

put into (a port), find out, turn out, call for, go down, aim at, depend on, regard as, adapt to, let loose, spur on, be devoted to, appeal to, get on with, mark off, rely on

2.

charge, appeal, just, close, figure, regard

3.

1 “There are so many inventions,” Jorkens said, “of which we know nothing.” (para. 4)

... the germ on which he was working seemed the most wonderful thing ever invented... (para. 23)

(In formal English, a relative pronoun is normally placed after the preposition when it's the object of the proposition.)

2 ... what if we know how to let loose a plague capable of destroying whole nations? (para. 16)

(“What if” introduces a question about what could happen or could have happened if something was or had been different.)

We knew that we had marvellous powers within our grasp, if only Carasierra could be kept at his work. (para. 18)

(“If only” is used to express unlikely desires. It is usually followed by a clause in the subjunctive mood.)

1... walked into a tavern to see if they had any decent wines... (para. 5)

(“If” in this sentence means “whether”. It's used to report an indirect yes-no question)

3 Once, if a nation had twelve battleships it was a Great Power... (para. 16)

(“Once” is an adverb meaning “at some time in the past, but not now.”)

4 Now that a man can carry in a bag a bomb that is more powerful than several battleships, it is hard to find out what any country can do or will do next. (para. 4)

“... now that I can do this,” he exclaimed. “I am going to make flowers.” (para. 43)

(The use of “now that” as a conjunction meaning “because”)

#### Reading Comprehension

1

Part I The introduction: Jorkens was asked what he thought the future of the world would be like. In answering the question, he recalled a story. (paras. 1–4)

Part II The body: Jorkens retold the story he heard from a man who used to work for the Ministry of Warfare of a small Caribbean country. (paras. 5–44)

1. Jorkens described how he met the man and made him tell the story.

2. The story the man told Jorkens.

1) The military ambition of his country: domination of the whole Caribbean.

2) Their attempt to realize the ambition.

a. They got rid of their Minister of Warfare.

b. They adopted modern warfare, depending on the intelligence/wisdom of scientists.

c. They tried to make the brilliant scientist, Carasierra, invent a deadly germ.

d. They had Carasierra executed when he turned his mind to inventing a living blade of grass.

Part III The conclusion of Jorkens' story: The man with a mustache fell silent as if still thinking about what his country almost had achieved. (para. 45)

2

1. There are three stories in the text, told by the author, Jorkens, and the man with a black mustache respectively. The one told by the man with a black mustache is the main story. This structure creates a multi-story set equivalent to a Russian doll.

2. Jorkens was probably respected and acknowledged as well-informed and knowledgeable in the club, that's why people asked for his opinion. Men were talking about world affairs in the club. Given that the story was written in the first half of the 20th century when only wealthy people could afford to travel overseas, Jorkens was probably from a wealthy background. Well-travelled and well-informed, he was a man of vision.

3. Jorkens didn't know what was going to happen in the world, because the technological innovations had accelerated the change around the world beyond people's imagination.

No one could predict other countries' actions given the emerging inventions.

The transition is natural and clever. Notice that in the first four paragraphs Jorkens' words are enclosed in quotation marks, while in paragraph 5, the quotation marks disappear, which suggests that this is a new narrative.

4. Jorkens went ashore in a Caribbean port and looked for some decent wines in a local tavern where he ran into the man with black mustache. A certain look in the man's eyes aroused Jorkens' interest, so he bought the man a drink. Under the influence of alcohol, the man began to talk. It's ridiculous for a government official to take a drink from a stranger and disclose a state secret to him without hesitation. Besides, Jorkens didn't believe that there were any decent wines in this small Caribbean country. All this may revealed Jorkens' prejudice and sense of superiority.

5. The man worked with the Ministry of Warfare. He was possibly the minister as he said “we responsible ministers” in paragraph 44. There is no country whose government has a department of war. It's usually called department/ministry of defense.

There may be two reasons why the author doesn't use the common name. First, the unusual name suggests that this is just a story. Second, that the country has a ministry of warfare rather than a defense ministry suggests that the government of this small Caribbean country is ambitious and aggressive. It's not a surprise that such a chauvinistic government would resort to bioweapons of mass destruction to fulfill its dream.

6. More destructive weapons would appear and change the balance of power. With advanced technology and new inventions, small countries would have opportunities to challenge traditional powers. He thought it's just because it was in the nation's interest.

7. He could invent a deadly germ that would destroy a whole nation. No, he didn't do it.

8. Another invention engaged his interest. He probably knew the consequences. Other possible reasons: he thought that it was morally wrong to invent a bioweapon of mass destruction, he hated to be coerced, he didn't think that science should serve a practical need.

9. He was interested in making a blade of grass because it was more wonderful than making a deadly germ and a scientist should work for wonder.

10. Carasierra was killed. The man had Carasierra executed because the latter refused to serve the government. Besides, a minster should walk the walk.

11. Jorkens told the story in the club to prove his point that the world was unpredictable and that there was no telling what one country was capable of doing. It's a good point. Though Jorkens seemed to poke fun at the man with black mustache and the aborted plan of the country, he did point out a new possibility: technological and scientific development could empower a country regardless of its size.

12. For the scientist: the invention of the blade of artificial grass.

For the man with black mustache: the invention of the deadly germ.

13. In a sense, it's a fable. It describes the realistic situation of the world. New weapons of mass destruction have been invented, which could disrupt the current world order. With new weapons, a handful of individuals could destroy the humanity. Another possible message of the story is that any attempt to conquer the world by immoral means is doomed. Besides, the author seemed to condemn the reality that science had been held hostage by politics. Students are encouraged to interpret the story as long as they can justify their interpretation.

1. A great invention may serve the interest of the humanity or a certain nation. Students may support their views with examples in history.

# Unit 10

## Text A · What Happiness Is · Eduardo Porter

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

The pursuit of happiness is an inalienable right for all human beings. Governments today usually vow to promote the well-being of their people. However, increasing reports seem to indicate that many people are not feeling any happier in spite of the material abundance in many countries including the United States. To solve this problem, the first thing we can do is to find out the scientific definition of happiness before taking any action. Eduardo Porter decided to take a stab at this question in this essay.

He states that "happiness is a slippery concept" and that it's impossible to give a precise and stable definition. People interpret happiness from various perspectives and therefore its meaning varies from person to person, and from time to time. People often take it as the day-to-day immediate pleasure, as especially the case in a quick-fix society, and lose sight of the lifelong pursuit of happiness. Many people only regard such kind of "gut reactions" as the source of happiness and pay little attention to the in-depth analysis of what happiness really is.

Apart from the psychological analysis, Porter also offers explanations from an economic angle. Although we constantly make puzzling choices that run against our ultimate goal of happiness, we do seek what we think will make us happy. Economists believe that the pleasure principle drives us to work and make money. The possession of worldly goods makes us happy. Obsession with GDP also serves as a reflection of our pursuit of happiness.

#### 2 Structure

The text can be roughly divided into two parts, which can be further divided into several sections.

**Part I Happiness is difficult to define and measure. (paras. 1–5)**

1. Happiness is a slippery concept that can be interpreted in many ways. (para. 1)
2. There is an agreed understanding of "subjective well-being" among psychologists and economists. (para. 2)
3. There are also some objective measures of people's quality of life. (para. 3)
4. Happiness is difficult still to measure, and people's answers to the question about happiness differ/change/vary from time to time. (paras. 4–5)

**Part II People pursue happiness in different ways according to their own perception of it. (paras. 6–9)**

1. The economic approach to happiness defines it as "utility". (para. 6)
2. There are limitations in the economic approach as can be seen in people's puzzling choice. (para. 7)
3. Lincoln's story suggests that people pursue happiness according to how they perceive/feel about it. (para. 8)
4. Economic growth cannot be dismissed in people's pursuit of happiness despite its negative side effects because people believe it makes them happy/improves their well-being. (para. 9)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1) Porter begins the essay by saying that "happiness is a slippery concept". We know that he is going to discuss the idea of happiness, a subject we are all interested in, but we all take it for granted that we know all about it. We naturally feel curious about what the author has got to say that is new to us.

2) Our first question perhaps would be why Porter says "Happiness has no precise, stable definition". He gives three examples, which may remind us of other countless examples. Some find that their happiness lies in the selfless concern for other people's happiness and welfare: for the Tang dynasty poet Du Fu, happiness is the wish to provide shelter for all that need it; for the Song dynasty statesman Fan Zhongyan, his happiness comes only after everyone else finds enjoyment; for many sincere revolutionary fighters like Li Dazhao, happiness means to fight for the glorious mission of liberating humanity, and to avoid the distractions of unsubstantial ideas and superficial fame. Others believe their happiness resides in choosing the lifestyle that suits them: for Chinese peasants, they once defined happiness as a life "with the ownership of 3 mu of land, and a cow, plus a wife, children and a warm kang"; for Liu Yuxi, he is content and happy to live in a simple dwelling, following a life that is refined in culture and learning, of which we can get a glimpse in his prose-poem "The Scholar's Humble Dwelling"; for Henry Thoreau, his happiness is to conduct an experiment by Walden Pond and to gain an understanding of the wisdom of life. We will perhaps resort to consulting a dictionary, and we find it defines happiness as gladness, joy, pleasure, well-being, satisfaction, contentment, elation and so on, none of which is any more precise or stable. By then we are likely to feel convinced that the whole discussion is a real challenge.

3) The author provides many possible definitions of happiness in the essay: Is it a subjective well-being or objective? Is it equal to gut reactions or should it be based on thoughtful analysis? Is it a matter of enjoying our immediate pleasure or does it have to be achieved by realizing our long-term aspirations? If it is an objective concept, does it mean it can in a way be measured by objective factors? If so, does it mean it can be measured quantitatively like the weight, height or size of things in the physical world?

4) The author describes how psychologists, economists, behaviorists, politicians define happiness. We notice that in the author's opinion, they all make sense, but on the other hand, they all seem to have limitations or side effects. Perhaps that is why the author begins with the statement that happiness is a slippery concept. It means that we must look at the concept from all aspects, and we must not take a one-sided view and jump to conclusions.

5) It is hoped that Text B of this unit will be carefully studied as it eloquently challenges what the author regards as the wrong overemphasis on happiness.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

1. **Happiness is a slippery concept, a bundle of meanings with no precise, stable definition.** (para. 1)
Happiness is a good example of many concepts, especially in social sciences, that are difficult to define.
**slippery:** (informal) (of a situation, subject, problem, etc.) difficult to deal with and that you have to think about carefully
eg Freedom is a slippery concept (=because its meaning changes according to your point of view).
cf **slip:** (v) to slide a short distance by accident so that you fall or nearly fall
**slip:** (n) a small mistake, usually made by being careless or not paying attention
**slippers:** (n) loose, soft shoes that you wear in the house

2. **"Happiness is when what you think, what you say, and what you do are in harmony," proposed Gandhi. Abraham Lincoln argue "most people are about as happy as they make up their minds to be." Snoopy, the dog-philosopher in Peanuts, took what was to my mind the most precise stab at the problem. "My life has no purpose, no direction, no aim, no meaning, and yet I'm happy. I can't figure it out. What am I doing right?"** (para. 1)
**argue:** to give the reasons for your opinion, idea, belief, etc.
eg The senator argued for/in favor of/against making cuts in military spending.
Gandhi's remark seems to define happiness as the ability and the right to think, speak and act freely, and not as immediate pleasures or worldly possessions. This may be related to the role played by him in his time as well as the influence of the traditional Indian culture which focuses on afterlife. Lincoln's definition of happiness on the other hand stresses the importance of a person's aims, objectives and aspirations in this world. The last quotation seems to reflect the view of those who believe that the world owes them a living and happiness simply means to enjoy what life has to offer. This view has nothing to do with thoughtful analysis of human desires, aims, purposes, aspirations. The author says that of the three quotations he likes this one of the dog philosopher best, but most likely he is speaking with his tongue in his cheek. He is just being humorous.

3. **What am I doing right?** (para. 1)
I must have been doing something right. But I don't know what.

4. **Countries whose citizens are happier on average report lower levels of hypertension in the population.** (para. 3)
**on average:** typically; in general
eg On average, a person's income is highest when they are in their mid-50s.
The city's police chief says they arrest, on average, 80 people each day.
**hypertension:** high blood pressure
cf **hyper-:** (prefix) more than usual, often too much

5. **Happier people are less likely to come down with a cold.** (para. 3)
**come down with (sth):** to begin to have or suffer from (an illness)
eg I didn't go to work because I came down with the flu.

6. **People who say they are happier smile more often, sleep better, report themselves to be in better health, and have better relatives.** (para. 3)
This is a parallel structure. The verbs "smile", "sleep", "report", and "have" form four parallel phrases.

7. **And some research suggests happiness and suicide rates move in opposite directions.** (para. 3)
And some research indicates that the happier people are, the less likely it's for them to commit suicide.
**suggest:** to indicate; to put an idea into somebody's mind; to make somebody think that something is true
eg Recent studies suggest the possibility of a cure for the disease.
Some believe that organic foods offer no health benefits, but this research suggests otherwise.
The symptoms suggest a minor heart attack.

8. **Still, this concept can be difficult to measure.** (para. 4)
**measure:** to judge the importance, value or effect of something
eg The company never measures success in dollars alone.

9. **Just ask yourself how happy you are, say, on a scale of one to three.** (para.4)
**say:** let's say; let's suppose, for example
eg If funds start arriving in January, construction can begin in, say, June.
Someone with, say, between 300 and 500 acres could be losing thousands of pounds a year.
**scale:** a range of levels or numbers used for measuring something
eg to evaluate performance on a scale from 1 to 10

10. **Answers wander when people are confronted with these questions.** (para. 4)
**wander:** (of a person's mind or thoughts) drift, to stop being directed on something and to move without much control to other ideas, subjects, etc.
eg It's easy to be distracted and let your attention wander.
Try not to let your mind wander.

11. **And although we do seem to know what will make us happy in the future—fame, fortune, or maybe a partner, when we get to the future, it rarely does.** (para. 4)
The author is suggesting that people often believe that they would be happy if they become famous, rich, or are engaged in a stable relationship. But they may often be wrong. "Do" is used as a form of emphasis.
**rarely:** not often, seldom

12. **We mix up gut reactions with thoughtful analysis, and confuse immediate pleasure with our long-term aspirations.** (para. 4)
**gut reaction:** a reaction to a situation derived from a person's instinct. Here it refers to the sensation of immediate pleasure.

13. **During an experiment in the 1980s, people who found a dime on top of a Xerox machine before responding to a happiness survey reported a much higher sense of satisfaction with life than those who didn't.** (para. 5)
This is further proof that our gut reactions are often not the result of thoughtful analysis. This reminds us of a well-known story about how a group of monkeys who are unhappy because their master only offer three nuts in the morning and four in the evening, but are well-pleased when the master promises to give them four nuts in the morning and three in the evening. (Chinese idiom: 朝三暮四)

14. **Another study found that giving people a chocolate bar improved their satisfaction with their lives.** (para. 5)
These examples (including the one above) seem to indicate that people are generally more pleased by what they regard as an unexpected special bonus.
**bar:** a solid piece or block of material that is longer than it is wide
eg a bar of soap; a candy bar

15. **One might expect that our satisfaction with the entire span of our existence would be a fairly stable quantity—in spite of day-to-day joys and frustrations.** (para. 5)
Although we might feel happy at one moment for some reason and unhappy at another moment for another reason, we perhaps still picture that our life-long satisfaction will be relatively stable.

16. **Yet people often give a different answer to the same question about lifetime happiness if it is asked again one month later.** (para. 5)
The author's answer to the question above is no, and people's view of what makes them happy often does not last as long as a month.

17. **For if happiness is what people strive for, one needn't waste time trying to figure out what makes people happy.** (para. 6)
**strive:** to try very hard to achieve something
eg We encourage all members to strive for perfection.
**figure out:** to think about something until you understand it
eg I'm trying to figure out a way to help the poor.

18. **We gorge on chocolate despite knowing it will make us unhappy ten pounds down the road.** (para. 7)
**gorge on:** (sometimes disapproving) to eat a lot of something, until you are too full to eat any more
**down the road:** at some time in the future
eg There are certainly more and more new jobs waiting for young people down the road.
Practically everything has side effects, which will show up down the road.

19. **We are unable to distinguish between choices that have slightly different odds of making us happy.** (para. 7)
**odds:** the degree to which something is likely to happen
eg What are the odds he won't turn up?

**We herd, imitating successful behaviors around us.** (para. 7)
**herd:** to move somewhere as a group
cf **herd behavior:** herd behavior is a phenomenon in which individuals act collectively as part of a group, often making decisions as a group that they would not make as an individual.

20. **Legend has it that Abraham Lincoln was riding in a carriage one rainy evening, telling a friend that he agreed with economists' theory that people strove to maximize their happiness, when he caught sight of a pig stuck in a muddy riverbank.** (para. 8)
**legend has it that:** according to a legend
"Legend" is followed by an appositive clause (同位语从句), a clause that explains a noun, or shows the meaning of a noun.
**strive:** to try very hard to do something or to make something happen, especially for a long time or against difficulties
eg In her writing she strove for a balance between innovation and familiar prose forms.

21. **When the friend pointed out to a mud-caked Lincoln that...** (para. 8)
**a mud-caked Lincoln:** proper nouns can be preceded by the indefinite article "a" to indicate the special condition one is in.
eg A sad-faced Charles came in the meeting room.
We were met at the door by a smiling Mary, not by an angry Mary.

22. **What I did was perfectly consistent with my theory.** (para. 8)
My behavior just now was absolutely in agreement with my idea.
**be consistent with:** be in agreement with something
eg The results are entirely consistent with our earlier research.
These findings are consistent with other studies.

23. **If I hadn't saved the pig, I would have felt terrible.** (para. 8)
The past perfect tense is used here to express a past action or state in the subjunctive mood.

24. **So perhaps the proper response to those who deny the importance of economic growth is to agree that pursuing economic growth often has negative side effects—carbon emissions, environmental degradation—that are likely to make us unhappy down the road.** (para. 9)
**side effect:** an effect that is often unexpected or unwanted happening in addition to the main effect 副作用
Notice the main idea in the last two paragraphs is not to emphasize the side effects in our pursuit of happiness, but rather the idea that in spite of the side effects, those pursuits of happiness are still justified such as the goal for the growth of GDP. The pursuit of happiness often involves difficulties, hardships, heavy costs, sacrifices, even sufferings, but no pain, no gain, they are still worthwhile (within reason), because without them, we cannot realize our aspirations, which will make us happy.

### III Translation of Text A

幸福是什么

爱德华多·波特

幸福是个难以把握的概念，它有多重含义，无法给予精确而又固定不变的定义。很多思想家们都试图给幸福下定义。甘地曾说：“幸福即所想、所言、所为三者的和谐统一。”亚伯拉罕·林肯则认为：“大多数人十有八九都能按照自己的愿望获得幸福。”《花生漫画》里的哲学家小狗史努比的看法在我看来是最准确的，因为它直击了这个话题的本质。它说：“我的生活没有目标，没有方向，没有目的，没有意义，但是我很快乐。我不明白怎么回事，不知道我什么地方做对了？”

大多数研究幸福的心理学家和经济学家倾向于使用“主观安乐感”这一概念。他们认为该概念包含三个部分：自己在多大程度上能实现自我抱负的满足感，自己有多少诸如喜悦等的正面情感，以及自己没有多少类似愤怒等的负面情感。

幸福确实存在。它同人们对生活质量的客观衡量直接相关。一个国家国民整体幸福感越高，高血压的患病率就越低。幸福的人不易感冒，即便感冒了也能更快康复。对生活满意的人受伤了也能更快痊愈。幸福的人更常微笑，睡眠质量更高，自述感到更加健康，和亲戚的关系更加融洽。一些研究还表明幸福感和自杀率成反比，因为幸福的人不想死。

然而，幸福这个概念很难进行量化。你不妨用三分钟给你的幸福感打分，然后自问这分数代表的意义。这时人们的回答就糟透了。我们通常会把本能反应和经过深思熟虑的分析相混淆，也常常把眼前的快感与人生的长远目标相混淆。我们也许会认为，我们知道将来什么能给我们带来幸福：名誉、财富、或许加上理想的伴侣。但当我们真的到达将来时，发现往往并非如此。

二十世纪八十年代的一个实验发现，实验开始前在复印机上捡到一角硬币的受试者比没有捡到硬币的受试者幸福感更高。另外一项研究发现，一条巧克力也会提高人们对生活的满意度。也许有人认为我们一生中的满意度会是相对稳定的数字，不受每日喜怒哀乐的影响。但是对于是否幸福这个问题，人们经常每隔一个月就给出截然不同的答案。

弗洛伊德认为人“努力追求幸福，他们想要变得幸福并保持幸福”。大多数经济学家会同意幸福即经济学里的“实利主义”。这种说法成了他们的有力工具，以反对那些认为幸福的多少不取决于收入而决定于其他因素的人。如果幸福就是人们努力追求的目标，那么就不需要浪费时间去捉摸到底什么使人幸福了。我们只要看一个人平时在忙些什么即可。事实是大多数人选择在努力工作挣钱。这就表明，经济增长是我们追求幸福的结果，因为这使我们快乐。

这个解释也有不足之处。我们常常做一些并不会让自己更加幸福的决定。我们抽烟，虽然明明知道它会导致癌症和肺气肿。我们猛吃巧克力，虽然知道这样很快会发胖，让我们增加十磅体重，让自己不快乐。最近的盖洛普民意测验显示，约有三分之二的美国人自称超重，但只有四分之一的人说在努力减肥。上世纪八十年代，一个名为“预期理论”（又称为行为经济学）的学科采用心理学作为工具分析经济行为。他们发现了各种不符合标准经济学对幸福的解释的奇怪行为。例如，丢东西对我们幸福感的减损超过获得它的幸福感，这就是所谓的损失厌恶。我们无法区分哪些选择会让我们稍微多那么一点点幸福感。我们往往从为数不多的经历中匆忙总结出普遍但大多数情况下完全错误的结论。我们喜欢随大流，日后模仿旁人的成功行为。

但是，事实上我们总是追求自认为能给我们带来幸福的东西，虽然我们的一些选择也许不会给我们带来幸福，但是另一些的确会给我们带来幸福。据传说，一个雨夜，亚伯拉罕·林肯坐在马车上告诉他的朋友说，他赞同经济学家的理论，即人总是努力使幸福最大化。说话间他突然看见一头猪正被困在泥泞的河边。于是他命令马车停下，下车把猪拉出泥坑，拉到安全之处。朋友见他浑身是泥，就

## （续）

对他说，他为了救一头猪而不惜吃那么多苦头，正好推翻了他刚才的观点。林肯反驳说：“恰恰相反，我方才的所作所为正好印证了我的理论。因为如果我没有救那头猪，我会感到非常难受。”

所以，要回应那些否认经济增长的重要性的人，也许正确的做法是承认追求经济增长会带来消极的副作用，例如碳排放和环境恶化，从而使我们未来不幸福。但是，我们还是应该承认，美国大众和世界上多数人仍在花大量时间和精力获取更多的金钱和更高的国内生产总值，这也是事实。因为他们认为这可以让他们幸福。

### IV Key to Exercises

**Lead-in**

1. Answers to the question may vary according to diverse personal experiences. Encourage students to define "happiness" on their own.

2.

- Oxford English Dictionary defines "happiness" as "the state of feeling or showing pleasure."

- Longman Dictionary of Contemporary English defines "happiness" as "the state of having feelings of pleasure, for example because something good has happened to you or you are very satisfied with your life."

- Cambridge Dictionary defines "happiness" as "the feeling of pleasure or satisfaction."

People always understand the word happiness in different ways, as they take pleasure from different things or achievements.

It doesn't matter if we have diversified views towards the concept of happiness.

**Language Focus**

1.

1. Words with prefixes: disprove, discomfort
(More examples: dislike, disagree, dissatisfy, discover. All these words share the prefix "dis-" which means the opposite)

2. Words with verb suffixes: maximize, realize
(More examples: minimize, recognize, globalize, nationalize. All these words share the verb suffix "-ize", which is often translated as "化" in Chinese.)

2.

1. -mit:
from Latin mittere, meaning to let go – (to send out, send back, place under, send/put away, send in to, send/put together, let go, send across, let go at intervals)

2. -ject:
from Latin jacere, meaning to throw (throw under, throw against, throw out, throw back, throw forth, throw down, throw/put in, throw between)

3.

stick  v. [intransitive] to become fixed in one position and impossible to move
discipline  n. an area of knowledge
fit  v. to agree with
span  n. the length of time that sth. lasts or is able to continue
scale  n. a range of levels or numbers used for measuring sth.
bar  n. a solid piece of sth., rectangular in shape
figure (out)  v. to think about sb./sth. until you understand them/it
objective  adj. based on real facts and not influenced by personal beliefs or feelings

4.

Noun phrases:

quality of life, suicide rates, a happiness survey, a higher sense of satisfaction, the entire span of our existence, the fact of the matter, our pursuit of well-being, loss aversion, carbon emissions

Adjective-noun phrases:

a slippery concept, a precise definition, negative feelings, objective measures, opposite directions, gut reactions, thoughtful analysis, immediate pleasure, long-term aspirations, stable quantity, day-to-day joys and frustrations, lifetime happiness, economic growth, behavioral economics, environmental degradation

5.

1. It does exist. (para. 3)
And although we do seem to know…, it rarely does. (para. 4)
"do" used to make a statement stronger

2. Legend has it that… (para. 8)
Legend/rumor has it that… = According to a legend/rumor…

3. … he agreed with the economists' theory that people strive to maximize their happiness… (para. 8)
appositional clause (同位语从句), a clause that explains rather than describes

4. If I hadn't saved the pig, I would have felt terrible. (para. 8)
subjunctive mood (虚拟语气)

**Reading Comprehension**

1

Part I Happiness is difficult to define and measure. (paras. 1–5)

1. Happiness is a slippery concept that can be interpreted in many ways.
2. There is an agreed understanding of "subjective well-being" among psychologists and economists.
3. There are also some objective measures of people's quality of life.
4. Happiness is difficult still to measure, and people's answers to the question about happiness differ/change/vary from time to time.

Part II People pursue happiness in different ways according to their own perception of it. (paras. 6–9)

1. The economic approach to happiness defines it as "utility."
2. There are limitations in the economic approach as can be seen in people's puzzling choice.
3. Lincoln's story suggests that people pursue happiness according to how they perceive/feel about it.
4. Economic growth cannot be dismissed in people's pursuit of happiness despite its negative side effects because people believe it makes them happy/improves their well-being.

2

1. Happiness is composed of three parts: feeling satisfied…, having feelings such as joy that make us see what is good in life/a situation, and not having feelings like anger that make us see what is bad in life/a situation.

2. When people are asked these questions, the answers do not present themselves immediately; instead, they are hard to find and often seem to mix with other matter.

3. You might think that in the complete length of time in which we live, our satisfaction with life should remain basically the same, even though we might feel happy or unhappy on different days.

4. We eat a lot of chocolate until we are too full to eat any more, even though we know that it will make us unhappy in the future as it will make us ten pounds heavier.

5. For example, losing something makes us so much unhappier than winning the same thing that we focus on trying to avoid loss more than on trying to make gains.

3

1. The author's purpose of writing this essay is to clarify the concept of happiness. He has succeeded in answering the question by showing to the readers that people interpret happiness from various perspectives and therefore its meaning varies from person to person, and from time to time.

2. The author begins the essay by saying that "happiness is a slippery concept." We know that he is going to discuss the idea of happiness, a subject we are all interested in, but we all take for granted that we know all about it. The key sentence in the whole essay is "happiness is a slippery concept." I agree with the author that happiness is a slippery and unstable concept, and the reason why the author bothers to write this essay is that increasing reports seem to indicate many people are not feeling any happier in spite of the material abundance in many countries including the United States. It is important to reach a good understanding of this concept in order not to get lost in the pursuit of material gains.

3. The author quotes Gandhi, Abraham Lincoln, and Snoopy to show the rich variety of answers to the question "What happiness is?". The implied meanings of the quotations are that many thinkers have interpreted the concept from different perspectives, and they all have justified their ideas. All in all, happiness is not a stable concept.

4. Most psychologists and economists prefer to call happiness "subjective well-being," as a person who has a high level of satisfaction with their life, and who experiences a greater positive affect and little or less negative affect, would be deemed to have a high level of "subjective well-being" or in simpler terms, be very happy. By "subjective," they mean it is hard to be measured by the facts and is always influenced by or based on personal beliefs or feelings.

5. Happiness can relate to objective measures of people's quality of life, as people who are happier on average report better data in health, such as being less likely to come down with a cold or to commit suicide.

6. Happiness can be difficult to measure, and the author concludes so for the following two reasons: people's answers to how happy they are would always drift; people are often imagining that they would be happy if something occurs to them, but they may often be wrong.

7. The author quotes Sigmund Freud in paragraph 6 in order to compare "happiness" to "utility." This theory helps us understand the concept of "happiness" from an economic approach. This theory also supports the pursuit of economic growth by rebutting the proposal of those who argue people should measure happiness not by income but by something else. The fact that people mostly choose to work and make money means economic growth is the outcome of our pursuit of well being. It is what makes us happy.

8. The limitations of Sigmund Freud's approach to happiness is that people often make puzzling choices that do not make them consistently happier. They often base themselves on a few experiences to arrive at broad, mostly wrong conclusions.

9. The author tells the story about Lincoln in order to persuade the readers that we pursue what makes us feel happy. The happiness of being able to save the pig outweighs the trouble Lincoln took to save it. This proved that happiness is doing things that will make you feel happy, and avoid the discomfort of not doing good things, which makes us feel terrible.

10. After reading the text, I have reached a better understanding of happiness, which is both subjective and objective. It should be a long-life pursuit, yet people are always disguised by immediate satisfaction. It is not the same as pleasure, as pleasure. Being happy does not always mean to be good, yet to be good to others can make people happy.
The author's conclusion is that happiness is a slippery concept, and pursuing money or a bigger GDP will not always lead to a happy life.

**Unit project**

1. Prompts:

Students are encouraged to narrow down the topic by choosing one of the two statements.

The 3-minute presentation should include the interpretation of the statement, and personal experience or concrete examples to support the interpretation, and a convincing summary to wrap it up.

2. Prompts:

Chinese traditionally describe happiness as "Harmony." Chinese students characterize happiness as a "harmonious balance (or equilibrium)" within the self, as well as between the self and his environment.

Here are three popular Chinese quotes about happiness:

- To know contentment is to be happy always. (知足常乐)
- Happy is the man without sickness. （无病是福）
- Being charitable is the happiest. （为善最乐）

# Unit 11

## Text A · Christmas Day in the Morning · Pearl S. Buck

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

"Christmas Day in the Morning" is intended to inspire people to have high ideals and noble sentiments. Because the characters and the plot are simple and down-to-earth, the story can appeal to a mass audience. Many ordinary people can identify with the hero. They might see themselves as the same type of person, and they might imagine themselves as giving a loved one a similar special gift. For these reasons, "Christmas Day in the Morning" is a story that can be found in certain popular magazines in North America. The magazine Collier's, where the story was originally published, no longer exists, but such stories often appear in the pages of Reader's Digest which, as introduced in the previous unit, has an extremely large circulation.

The central idea of this story is expressed in the statement that love alone can waken love. It means that love is always mutual and always begins with giving. In view of the growing lack of human warmth partly due to fierce competition in modern society, this idea appears all the more important. Of course it would be naive to think that love is the answer to all our problems today, and in a world divided by class, racial and national conflicts, universal love is still unrealistic.

The story contains flashbacks. Draw the student's attention to the points where the time setting is shifted. The style is simple and clear. No big words. No involved sentences. No pompous "purple" passages. This is the kind of style that inspires a sense of sincerity and honesty. The students should be encouraged to take this as a model. To help the students better understand the text, it might be a good idea to give a little background about Christmas and the birth of Jesus Christ, and also point out that the story is written to convey the idea of love.

#### 2 Structure

The text can be roughly divided into three parts, which can be further divided into several sections.

**Part I The introduction: the magic of Christmas for Robert as an old man (paras. 1–8)**

A. He woke up at four o'clock on Christmas Day and did not go back to sleep as usual. (paras. 1–2)

B. He insisted on a proper celebration for his wife and himself. (paras. 3–8)

**Part II The body: Robert's first gift of love for his father (paras. 9–41)**

A. A few days before Christmas when he was fifteen Rob realized how much his father loved him. (paras. 9–14)

B. The night before Christmas, Rob decided to do the milking on his own as a special gift for his father. (paras. 15–20)

C. On Christmas morning Rob got up an hour earlier than usual and got the milking done all by himself. (paras. 21–25)

D. His father said it was the best Christmas gift he had ever had. (paras. 26–41)

**Part III The conclusion: Robert's realization on the particular Christmas Morning (paras. 42–46)**

A. He realized love alone can waken love. (paras. 42–44)

B. He wrote a letter to his wife to express his love for her. (paras. 45–46)

#### 3 Teaching Tips

1) See if students have similar stories to tell about how their love for their parents or brothers and sisters was first awakened, and if they remember the first special gift they have ever given to their loved ones.

2) Ask the students to list the punch lines (画龙点睛之语) of the text. Have them discuss if they agree that love alone can awaken love.

- Does it mean love is always returned?
- Why is it that the popular belief nowadays seems to be that once you have money and power, you are sure to have love, and many pretty young women will be only too happy to throw themselves into your arms?
- What do you have to say about this?
- Is Pearl Buck being too idealistic?
- Is love the answer to all our problems?

3) Have students guess the time and cultural background of the story. Do they notice any details in the descriptions that indicate what time the story must have taken place?

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

**1. ... help with the milking. (para. 1)**

... help milk the cows.

**help with**: to give a hand in

**2. Strange how the habits of his youth clung to him still! (para. 1)**

It was strange how up to that moment he had continued doing things the way he had always done them from boyhood.

The complete form should be: It was strange how...

The elliptical form is often used in informal speech or narration.

More examples:

No wonder he lost his job.

Interesting how she came to know him.

Funny how it all started.

Question: What were the habits of his youth?

**3. Yet what was the magic of Christmas now? (para. 2)**

This is a negative statement in question form.

Another example: What's the use of going there?

This sentence shows that Christmas no longer had much attraction since they were old and there were only the two of them at home.

**4. His childhood and youth were long past, and his own children had grown up and gone. (para. 2)**

Question: What is meant by "his childhood and youth were long past"?

It means that he was no longer young.

Question: What is the meaning of "gone" here?

It means that they had left home.

**5. It isn't worthwhile, perhaps... (para. 3)**

Complete the sentence: ... perhaps we shouldn't bother about the celebration.

**6. Yes, Alice, even if there are only the two of us... (para. 4)**

Question: Did he agree with his wife?

No, he didn't agree with his wife. He felt that although their children were not coming, they should still have a proper Christmas.

**7. trim (para. 5)**

to decorate sth, especially around its edges 装饰；修饰

**8. Why did he feel so awake tonight? For it was still night, a clear and starry night. (para. 8)**

Question: Why did Rob call it "tonight" since the title is "Christmas Day in the Morning"?

The reason is given in the second sentence.

The sentence beginning with "For" is actually a coordinate clause of reason. It is turned into a sentence to make it sound like an afterthought!

**9. He slipped back in time, as he did so easily nowadays. (para. 9)**

He had recently got into the habit of recalling things in the past/of reminiscing/of letting his thoughts go back to the old days.

Question: Why did he easily slip back in time?

He was old now and old people tend to look backwards rather than forward because they often feel that they do not have much to look forward to in the future.

Question: What is the function of this sentence?

It introduces a flashback.

**10. He was fifteen years old and still on his father's farm... when he had overheard what his father was saying to his mother. (para. 9)**

on his father's farm: Note the use of "on" before the noun "farm".

He had not known it until one day...: The past perfect is used because the state of not knowing had lasted up to that day. "It" refers to the fact that he loved his father.

when: on which day. "When" here introduces an attributive clause.

overhear: to hear (sth spoken) without intention

**11. Mary, I hate to call Rob... I wish I could manage alone. (para. 10)**

I hate to call: I don't want to call... / I'm sorry that I have to call...

I wish I could manage alone: This is expressed in subjunctive mood, indicating that in fact he couldn't.

**12. His mother's voice was brisk, "... It's time he took his turn." (para. 11)**

brisk: quick, sharp and vigorous

take one's turn: to do one's share

"Took" is used here to indicate subjunctive mood. Notice the pattern "It's time sb + verb in the past tense".

**13. He had never thought of it before, taking for granted the tie of their blood. (para. 13)**

He had never thought of the fact that his father loved him because he took the relationship of father and son as a matter of course. He had never given this a thought.

**14. ... there would be no more loitering in the mornings and having to be called again. (para. 13)**

... he would never loiter in the morning and need no longer to be called again and again.

loiter: to move or go about business slowly and with many stops

**15. ... stumbling blind with sleep, and pulled on his clothes. (para. 13)**

The original text runs like this:

... stumbling blind with sleep, his eyes tight shut, but he got up and pulled on his clothes.

Question: What is the meaning of "stumbling blind with sleep"?

When he got up, it was still very dark and he was very sleepy. He could hardly open his eyes and could not see or think clearly, so he walked or moved unsteadily and blindly.

**16. And then on the night before Christmas,... And he always saved and bought them each something, too. (para. 14)**

This paragraph describes how Rob and the family usually celebrated Christmas. The way they celebrated Christmas revealed their poverty.

mince pie: a pie with a filling of mincemeat (a mixture of chopped apple, spices, raisins, etc. but no meat)

**17. the ten-cent store (para. 15)**

a store where everything is cheap, supposedly no more than ten cents

**18. He lay on his side and looked out of his attic window. (para. 15)**

lie on one's side: 侧身躺着

lie on one's back: 仰面躺着

lie on one's stomach: 趴着

Note: to lie, lied, lied 说谎；to lie, lay, lain 躺；to lay, laid, laid 放

**19. Then Jesus had been born in a barn, ... bringing their Christmas gifts! (para. 18)**

What were the Christmas gifts the Wise Men brought?

This is an allusion to the Bible:

... And when they had come into the house, they saw the young child with Mary his mother, and fell down, and worshipped him; and when they had opened their treasures, they presented

unto him gifts: gold and frankincense (乳香), and myrrh (没药)...

**20. A thought struck him like a silver dagger. (para. 19)**

An idea suddenly crossed his mind.

The simile of a silver dagger is used to suggest the lightning speed with which the brilliant idea flashed across his mind.

**21. He laughed to himself as he gazed at the stars. (para. 20)**

laugh to oneself: 暗自发笑

cf to laugh at sb 嘲笑他人

gaze: to look intently and steadily (as in wonder)

**22. ... he got up and crept downstairs, careful of the creaky boards... (para. 22)**

creep: to move slowly and quietly, especially with one's body close to the ground

creaky boards: the wooden planks which make harsh, grating or squeaking sounds, suggesting that the house is old and the boards are no longer firm

Question: Why did he have to be careful of the creaky boards?

He had to be careful so that the boards would not make any noise and wake up his father, thus spoiling his plan.

**23. The cows looked at him, sleepy and surprised. (para. 22)**

The cows seemed to him sleepy and surprised because this reflected his own feelings.

**24. ... two strong streams rushing into the pail, frothing and fragrant. (para. 24)**

froth: to cause to form a whitish mass of bubbles; to cause to foam

fragrant: sweet smelling

frothing and fragrant: alliteration (头韵)

**25. Milking for once was not a chore. (para. 25)**

For the first time milking was not a tedious, unpleasant and boring task.

**26. He put the stool in its place... (para. 25)**

He put the stool back in its original place...

**27. He put the covers over his head to silence his quick breathing. (para. 26)**

Question: Why did Rob put the covers over his head?

He did not want his father to hear his quick breathing and find out the truth.

**28. His dancing heart was ready to jump from his body. (para. 30)**

The word "dancing" is used here to suggest that he was very excited and his heart was beating fast and "dancing" with joy.

**29. The minutes were endless... (para. 31)**

The minutes seemed endless, because he was so eager to see how his father would react to his special gift. The feeling of the length of time is often connected with one's emotion.

**30. a queer sobbing sort of a laugh (para. 34)**

a strange kind of laugh like sobbing

His father was so moved that he was choked with emotion.

**31. His father was standing beside his bed, feeling for him... (para. 34)**

feel for sth: to try to find sth by touching; to grope for sth

**32. He found his father and clutched him in a great hug. He felt his father's arms go around him. (para. 36)**

Words like "found" and "felt" indicate that it was dark and they couldn't see each other.

clutch: to hold tightly

hug: a close, affectionate embrace

**33. His heart was bursting with love. (para. 38)**

His heart was filled/overflowing with love.

**34. Come to think of it... (para. 39)**

(informal) I've never thought about it before, but now when I think about it, I realize that...

More examples:

We must hurry. Come to think of it, we only have two days left.

I normally write home once every month. Come to think of it, I should write to my mother today.

**35. Outside the window now the stars slowly faded. (para. 42)**

Question: Why is the word "now" used here?

It is used here to indicate the switching back of time.

**36. Love alone could waken love. (para. 44)**

Only love could waken love. Love could not be bought or forced.

### III Translation of Text A

圣诞节的早晨

赛珍珠

他突然醒了，完全醒了。时间是早晨四点钟，正是他父亲过去经常叫他起床帮忙挤奶的时间。奇怪的是这种青年时代养成的习惯竟然一直保持到现在！他父亲已去世三十年了，而他仍然是一到早晨四点钟就醒。但是今天早晨，因为是圣诞节，他不想接着睡了。

现在的圣诞节没有什么特别魅力呢？他的童年和青年时期已经过去很久了，他的几个孩子也都长大成人离开他了。

昨天他妻子说："圣诞节不值得那么张罗了，也许……"

而他说："不，值得的。艾丽斯，即使只有我们两个人，我们也应该过个自己的圣诞节。"

接着，她说："罗伯特，我们明天再装饰那棵圣诞树吧，我累了。"

他同意了，所以那棵圣诞树还在后门外。

他躺在自己房间的床上。她的房门关着，因为她一向睡得很轻。几年前他们就决定分开睡了，两人的睡眠都没以前好了。他们结婚这么久了，已经没有什么可以分开他们的。

为什么今晚他那么清醒呢？因为现在还是夜晚，一个满天星斗的夜晚。当然，那晚没有月亮，但是星光却不同寻常！现在他想起圣诞节黎明前的星星好像总是又大又亮。

他的思绪回到了从前，近来他常常这样。那年他十五岁，仍住在父亲的农场里。他爱父亲。但那是在某个圣诞节前的某一天，在他偶然听到父亲对母亲说的话后才意识到这一点。

"玛丽，我真不愿天天一大早就把罗布叫起来。他正在长个子，需要睡眠。我自己一个人干得了就好了。"

"问题是你一个人不行，亚当，"母亲说得很干脆，"再说他已不是孩子了，也该轮到他干活了。"

"嗯，"父亲慢慢地说，"可我实在不愿叫醒他。"

父亲的这番话使他突然明白：父亲爱他！他以前从未想过这一点，总把这一切都看作是理所当然的血缘关系。现在他既然知道了父亲爱他，以后早晨就不能再磨磨蹭蹭，非得让父亲再叫他。于是他马上起床，带着睡意，跌跌撞撞地穿上了衣服。

在圣诞节前夜，他躺在床上想着第二天的事。他家很穷，圣诞节最激动人心的就是他们自己养的火鸡和母亲做的苹果馅饼。他的妹妹们会自己缝制一些礼物。他们的父母则会买些他们需要的东西，比如一件暖和的夹克，或者书什么的。他也总会攒点钱，给他们每人买点东西。

可是就在他十五岁的那年圣诞节，他希望能给父亲一件好一点的礼物，而不是仅从便宜商店里买来的普通领带。他侧身躺着，从阁楼的窗子里往外望。

记得小时候他问过："爸爸，马厩是什么？"

"就是牲口棚，"父亲回答说，"像我们家的那个一样。"

## （续）

这么说来那是在牲口棚里出生的，那些牧羊人和贤人们带着给耶稣的诞生礼物就是到牲口棚来看他的。

这时一个念头猛地闪过他的脑海。他为什么不在牲口棚里给父亲送一件特别的礼物呢？他可以早点起来，悄悄溜进牲口棚，把挤奶的活儿全干了。这样等父亲进去挤奶时，他会发现活儿全干完了。

他凝望着星星，暗自发笑。就这么做，不过他绝对不能睡得太死了。

他肯定醒着有二十次，每次都划一根火柴看看他那块旧表。

三点差一刻时他起了床，悄悄下楼，小心翼翼地不让楼板发出吱吱的声音，然后来到了外面。一颗硕大的星星低悬在屋顶上，金光闪闪，带点红色。那些奶牛睡眼蒙眬，吃惊地看着他。对它们来说这时也太早了。

但它们平静地接受了他。他给每头奶牛都拿了些干草，然后取来奶桶和大奶罐。

以前他从来没有独自一人挤过奶，不过感觉好像挺容易的。他微笑着，稳稳地挤着，两股牛奶流入奶桶，泛起了泡沫，散发出芳香。奶牛们都十分听话，好像它们也知道那天是圣诞节一样。

活儿进行得比以往任何时候都要顺利。他第一次觉得挤奶是这么轻松，这是他献给父亲的礼物。他挤完了奶，两个大奶罐全满了；他把它们盖好，小心翼翼地关上奶房的门，并确认把门闩好了。他把凳子放回门边，又把干净的奶桶挂好。

然后，他走出牲口棚，闩上大门。

他回到自己的卧室里，刚刚脱下衣服跳上床就听见父亲起床了。他用被子蒙住头，盖住自己急促的呼吸声。门开了。

“罗布！”父亲喊道，“该起床了，儿子，虽说今天是圣诞节。”

“好——的，”他故意睡意蒙眬地答道。

“我先走了，”父亲说，“我先去干起来。”

门关上了。他一动不动地躺着，暗自微笑。几分钟后父亲就会知道的。他的心狂跳着，都快跳出嗓子眼儿来了。

这几分钟时间显得太长了——十分钟，十五分钟，他也不知道过了多少分钟——最后他终于听见父亲的脚步声。门开了。

“罗布！”

“怎么啦，爸爸——”

“好你个臭小子——”父亲笑着说，但他笑得很怪，带点哽咽的声音。“你以为你能骗我，是吗？”父亲站在他床边，摸索着找他，掀开了他的被子。

“爸爸，这是给您的圣诞节礼物！”

他摸到父亲，紧紧地搂住他。他感到父亲的双臂也抱住了他。天仍很黑，他们看不见彼此的脸。

“孩子，谢谢你。没有比这更好的礼物了——”

“哦，爸爸，我想让您知道——我真的想做好孩子！”这些话不自觉就自己蹦出来了。他不知道说什么才好，他的心中充满着爱。

“哦，我想我可以回去睡了，”过了一会儿父亲说，“不，听——弟弟妹妹们还在睡觉呢。说起来，孩子，我还从来没有看见过你们第一眼见到圣诞树的那种快乐样子，因为我那时总在牲口棚里。来吧！”

罗伯特又穿上衣服，与父亲一起来到圣诞树前。不久太阳便慢慢爬了上来，到了刚才那颗星星的地方。啊，多么美丽的圣诞清晨啊！当父亲告诉母亲，他，罗布，今天怎样自己起床独自把奶挤好时，他又一次满心感到害羞和自豪。

“这是我得到过的最好的圣诞礼物。孩子，我会记住它的，只要我活着，每年圣诞节早晨我都会记起它。”

他和父亲都一直记着这件事。现在父亲已经去世，就剩下他一人记着此事了：在那个幸福的圣诞早晨，他独自与奶牛在牲口棚里，准备了表达自己真爱的第一件礼物。此刻，窗外的星星慢慢地隐退了。他下了床，穿上拖鞋和浴衣，轻轻踮脚下楼，把圣诞树搬进了屋子，开始仔细地修剪树。很快就能弄好了。然后，他来到书房，取出一个小盒子，里面装着他送给妻子的特别礼物，一枚钻石胸针。胸针不大，但设计精美。他觉得这还不够令他满意，他想告诉她——告诉她他是多么爱她。

他一生中一直保持着爱人的心，这是多么幸运的事！啊，能够爱他人，这才是人生快乐的真正所在！因为他确信有些人真的不能爱任何人。而他心中充满着爱，现在仍然如此。

他突然想到，他之所以至今爱心未灭，就是因为多年前父亲对他的爱唤醒了他心中的爱。正是这样：世上只有爱才能唤醒爱。

今天早晨，在这个幸福的圣诞早晨，他要把爱献给他亲爱的妻子。他要把这份爱写在信中，让她去读，去永远保存。他走到书桌边，开始写道：“我最亲爱的……”

写完后，他封好信，把它系在圣诞树上。然后关上灯，踮着脚尖上楼。天上的星星渐渐消失，东方已经露出第一缕晨光。多么幸福、美妙的圣诞节啊！

### IV Key to Exercises

#### Language Focus

1

Notes:

1. words changed into different part of speech through conversion and shift of word stress
2. Many verbs in English have the verb suffix “-ate,” which is a “dead suffix,” dead in the sense that it is no longer used freely to form new verbs, but it is still worth knowing, such as celebrate, cooperate, appreciate, translate, investigate, evaluate, participate, imitate, complicate, frustrate, tolerate, integrate.
3. “-ish” can be used as adjective, verb, and noun suffixes.
4. Prefix “a-” can be used to make adjectives or adverbs.

2

1. burst with (love), cling to, gaze at, (the sun) creep up, hang up, make sure of, occur to sb, pull on/off one’s clothes, put sth. in its place, slip back in time
2. all by oneself, come to think of it, for once, on the farm

3

bar the door, fool a person, milk the cow, seal a letter, sew clothes, silence one’s breathing, strike a match, take one’s turn, trim the tree, waken love

4

1. It was four o’clock, the hour at which his father had always called him to get up and help with the milking. (para. 1)
（同位语 appositive）
2. “… I wish I could manage alone.” (para. 10)
（虚拟语气 unreal condition/subjunctive mood）
3. The cows were behaving well, as though they knew it was Christmas. (para. 24)
（as though/if 虚拟语气 unreal condition/subjunctive mood）
4. “… I do want to be good!” (para. 38)
（do表示强调 do in the emphatic structure）
5. … I’ll remember it, son, every year on Christmas morning, as long as I live.” (para. 41)
（as long as/so long as/on condition that/provided (that) 条件句 conditional）
6. … now that his father was dead he remembered it alone… (para. 42)
（now that/since原因状语从句——表示原因语气较弱，弱于because。adverbial clause of cause/reason）
7. We have to get up, son, even if it is Christmas. (para. 27)
（even if/though 让步状语从句 An adverbial clause of concession）

#### Reading Comprehension

1

(1) children (2) wife (3) celebrate (4) four (5) milking
(6) clung (7) recalled (8) overheard (9) parents (10) awakened
(11) decide (12) all by himself/on his own/alone (13) chore
(14) proud (15) gift (16) remembered (17) love
(18) joy (19) express

#### Language Exercises

1 Expand your vocabulary.

1.

1) alike
2) live; alive
3) shameful; ashamed
4) asleep; sleeping
5) awake; waking

(Note: Prefix “a-” is often used to form adjectives or adverbs when added to nouns or adjectives, and it often means “on” or “in,” or is used as an intensifier (作加强语气用). For example: ahead, aboard, ashore, abreast, alive, alike, ashamed, aside, asleep, awake, afar. These words are usually used as predicative adjectives, not as attributive adjectives.)

2.

1) simplified
2) authorized, organize
3) normalized
4) industrialized, urbanized
5) modernization, classified
6) legalized, justified, civilized
7) intensify, unification
8) nationalized
9) notified, computerized
10) globalization, idealized

Note: “-ize” (BrE: -ise) and “-fy” are common verb suffixes. They mean “to cause to become” and “to form into.” For example: privatize, beautify.

2

1. 赶快走/飞快冲进（出）房间
2. 偷偷溜进（出）房间

5. 昂然/跳跃着进（出）房间
6. 费力地挤到队伍前面
7. 用手推开人群/用肩膀挤开人群/用胳膊肘挤开人群
