14. 杀出重围回到自己的队伍
15. 火箭式地升到了权力巅峰

3

1. in, in
2. of, of, off
3. with, to, as
4. to, as
5. Unlike, for
6. in, off, off, of/about/with
7. as, to, up
8. in, with

4

1. It struck me as odd how a young man of twenty could have such deep thoughts.
2. Many of those beautiful dresses are designed by young people, who seem to be bursting with new ideas.
3. He was a retired general, but he still clung to the habit of jumping out of bed and slipping into his uniform in the middle of the night as if to inspect the soldiers on duty.
4. I think we must all realize that there is no free lunch. We can’t take anything for granted. We must be ready to pay for what we get.
5. My money for this month is all gone. I must ask my father for more. But when I come to think of it, it is a shame that I still have to depend on my parents.
6. Now that the public’s environmental awareness increases, our living environment is getting better and better nowadays.
7. How did they manage to dig that long tunnel to bring the water from the other side of the mountain all the way to this area?

5

1. worth
2. worth/while
3. worthy
4. worth
5. nowadays
6. now
7. nowadays, now
8. look
9. gaze/stare
10. staring
11. glanced
12. received, accepted
13. accept
14. received
15. lovely, beloved/loving
16. lovely, beloved (often humorous)/loving (not lovely for men; say “handsome” or “good-looking”)
17. cry
18. crying, sobbing
19. wept
20. weep, weep (from a poem by Ella Wilcox)

6

1. as though
2. before
3. as long as
4. even if
5. Not that, even if
6. if only, Now that
7. for once
8. If only, only if
9. until after
10. Now that

7 (for reference only)

1. 1) you success/luck
2) I had more time
3) I were ten years younger
2. 1) everybody is here
2) we are college students
3) we have become one of the world’s largest economies
3. 1) we put more emphasis on environmental protection
2) we paid more attention to critical thinking
3) I got myself married
4. 1) like fish
2) did go to the talk
3) did smoke a lot in the past
(Note: This exercise expects students to be more creative, therefore what is given here as the key is actually a mere suggestion.)

8

1. Don’t take it for granted that once we get into a college, we are sure to succeed.
2. It occurred to us that if we allowed more and more of our farmland to be used for other purposes, perhaps someday we might not be able to feed our nation.
3. Strange that many of us still tend to cling to some past bad habits.
4. Our hearts were nearly all bursting with pride when we learned that we had finally succeeded in setting up our first space station on the moon.
5. It’s high time we young people took our turn (in running the country).
6. It wasn’t until after he had removed his sunglasses that I realized that I had mistaken him for my good friend.
7. Galileo knew that it took courage to challenge the authority of Aristotle, but he was determined to publish his new discovery even if he risked becoming everybody’s laughingstock.
8. “As long as I’m in charge,” said the president of the university, “I will make sure that the teachers’ working conditions improve steadily.”

9

(1) which (2) that/which (3) what (4) teach (5) control
(6) group (7) at (8) could (9) shared (10) ideas

10

1. People in the 21st century no longer cling to the idea that men are ~~more~~ superior to women.
2. I was simply unable to go anywhere during the holidays because I had so many important things to attend to including a term paper which was two weeks overdue.
3. It never occurred to me how my great grandmother could manage to raise six children single-handedly and send them all to college.
4. ~~Since~~/Now that he had decided to get the milking done all by himself the following morning, he knew he must not oversleep. Therefore, that night he must have woken up a dozen times to check his watch.
5. Bursting with gratitude, none of us could just leave without saying goodbye to the villagers.
6. —We must not take it for granted that an ideal world will be presented to us on a silver platter.
—No. We must all strive for it. And it’s not going to be smooth sailing either.
7. It wasn’t until about midnight that I heard my son open the door and creep ~~hurriedly-and-noisily~~ upstairs into his room.
8. Opinions differ. Some people think this destroyed royal park is worth restoring, but others think we’d better ~~to~~ keep it as it is as a reminder of history.

---

# Unit 12

## Text A · The Monsters Are Due on Maple Street · Rod Serling

### I Introduction

#### 1 Background

**1. Genre**
This text is written in the tradition of science fiction. Science fiction usually describes imaginary future developments in science. But unlike *Round the World in 80 Days* by Jules Verne, today's science fiction often tries to give a moral message. Therefore, they are more "fiction" than "science". The present text is a good example.

**2. Elements of drama**
This is a short two-act play. In reading a play, first we should learn about the setting or background, and get to know the characters and their relations to each other; then we must find out what is happening (the plot and the dramatic conflict), and understand how the drama leads to the climax or how the dramatic conflict is resolved.

**3. Language**
Plays are particularly good for learning spoken English. Students should pay particular attention to the pronunciation and intonation, as well as such special features of oral English as contractions, ellipses and colloquial expressions. Students are strongly advised to act out the whole play.

**4. Theme and thesis**
In the title of the play, the "monsters" seem to refer to those ETs (extraterrestrial beings) who are out to destroy us human beings on earth (the earthlings or earth people). When we finish the play, however, we realize that monsters actually live in our own hearts. Because of our weaknesses or wicked ways, we tend to destroy each other or seek our own destruction. This, and not the ETs, is our real enemy and real danger.

In this play, the author seems to emphasize three fatal human weaknesses: our deep suspicion and distrust of one another, our eagerness to find a scapegoat, and our readiness to turn into a mob. It implies that if we human beings want to be really safe, we must kill those monsters in our hearts: we should try to understand and trust each other, to be less eager to assign blame, and to resist the kind of group (mob) mentality which too often results in violence and tragedy.

#### 2 Structure
The teleplay can be divided into five parts on the basis of plot development, most of which can be further divided into several sections.

**Part I The introduction/exposition: The residents on Maple Street desperately try to understand what is happening in the neighborhood. (paras. 1–29)**
A. One late afternoon the residents on Maple Street hear a tremendous roar and see a flash of light across the sky. (paras. 1–2)
B. Strange things begin to happen and the residents try to figure out what is happening and some of them decide to go downtown to find out. (paras. 3–29)

**Part II The rising action: The conflict develops into one between the residents of Maple Street. (paras. 30–102)**
A. Tommy stops them saying all the strange things have been caused by monsters from outer space. (paras. 30–56)
B. Les Goodman is suspected of being an alien sent from outer space earlier, and when people move in on Goodman's house for action, they turn into a mob. (paras. 57–86)
C. Steve Brand begins to be suspected for trying to speak for Goodman. (paras. 87–102)

**Part III The crisis/climax: The conflict further intensifies when they use a shotgun. (paras. 103–111)**
A. An approaching figure frightens the residents. (paras. 103–107)
B. Charlie fires and he shoots a neighbor dead. (paras. 108–111)

**Part IV Falling action: The residents begin to quarrel and accuse each other. (paras. 112–134)**
A. Suspicion falls on Charlie, who puts all the blame on Tommy. (paras. 112–121)
B. People start to accuse each other. (paras. 122–134)

**Part V Resolution: The two creatures in the spacecraft forecast the destruction of the people on Maple Street, not by the monsters, but by themselves. (paras. 135–141)**

#### 3 Teaching Tips
1. Oral work
1) Have the students summarize the teleplay.
2) Ask them to make a character analysis of one of the following characters: Charlie, Steve, Tommy, or any other character.
3) Here are a few topics for discussion.

■ What message do you think Rod Serling intends to get across to the audience?

■ What destroys the people of Maple Street?

■ What do you think is the most dangerous human weakness?

2. Follow-up activities
1) Ask the students to perform the whole play or act out major events.
■ Have a play reading session in the class.

### II Detailed Discussion of the Text

**1. The Monsters Are Due on Maple Street (title)**
Question: Who are the monsters? Are they really responsible for all the troubles that happen on Maple Street? Where are the real monsters?
are due: to be expected to happen or arrive at a particular time
eg     The train is due in exactly 25 minutes.
        Their baby is due in October.
        She is due to arrive at 5:30.
        The meeting is due to start in 5 minutes.

**2. porch (para. 1)**
(AmE) a structure attached to the entrance of a house that has a roof and that may or may not have walls
Collocations: a front/back porch; an enclosed porch (=a porch that has walls)

**3. Steve Brand polishes his car parked in front of his house. (para. 1)**
parked in front of his house: past participle phrase used as an attributive qualifier
More examples:
a book written by Mark Twain
memory sticks made in Canada
an IMAX cinema opened last week
a 3-D movie produced in the US

**4. A Good Humor man (para. 1)**
a man who sells Good Humor products
Good Humor: an American brand of ice cream sold from ice cream trucks as well as stores. The Good Humor Company started in Youngstown, Ohio during the early 1920s and covered most of the country by the mid 1930s. At its peak in the 1950s, the company operated 2,000 "sales cars".

**5. ... and is just stopping to sell some ice-cream... (para. 1)**
stop to do sth: to stop (doing what you are doing in order) to do sth else
More examples:
She was working. When she saw me, she stopped to talk to me.
I worked the whole day. I only stopped to eat my meals.
I had to stop to ask direction many times.

**6. a couple of (para. 1)**
two of the same kind or a small number of
More examples:
a couple of days/weeks/months/years, a couple of people, another/first/next/last couple of hours/weeks, etc

**7. gossip (para. 1)**
to spend time talking to somebody about other people's behavior and private lives or about other things that do not concern oneself

**8. Another man waters his lawn. (para. 1)**
Like "water", the following nouns can also be used as verbs:
land, screw, face, back, grade, score, question, fish, book, ship, paper, truck, bike, dust, etc
More examples:
The workers are loading goods from a ship.
She slowly backed the car into the garage.
Before we move in we have to paper the room first.
Would you please book a ticket for me?

**9. a tremendous roar (para. 2)**
a terrible continuous loud noise, especially made by animals like lions, or by a man or a machine

**10. A flash of light plays on his face... (para. 2)**
A flash of light shines on and moves about on his face...

**11. ... the man... stands there speechless. (para. 2)**
"Speechless" here is used as the subject complement denoting the state "the man" is in. It is not used to modify the verb "stands".
More examples:
The sun was burning hot.
He was lying there, fast asleep.
Three months later, she came back home, penniless.

**12. from across the street (para. 2)**
The preposition "from" can be followed by another preposition. Draw students' attention to the use of such double prepositions.
More examples:
He jumped from behind the tree.
She drew a gun from inside the drawer.
I came from beyond the mountains.

**13. Guess it was a meteor, honey. Came awful close, didn't it? (para. 7)**
Ellipsis is common in speaking. In both of the sentences above, the subject is dropped. "Guess it was..." should be "I guess..." and "Came awful close..." should be "It came awful close..."

**14. We see a man screwing in a light bulb on a front porch... (para. 8)**
Let students imagine themselves reporting to the police what they have seen or heard in a serious fight which finally led to a murder case.
They may use the following expressions:
I saw sb do/doing sth
I heard sb do/doing sth
I found sb doing sth

**15. ... finding that nothing happens. (para. 8)**
... finding that the light is not on.
nothing happens: used to say that what you do does not bring about a result you expect
More examples:
I knocked at the door for five minutes, but nothing happened. (=There was no answer.)
I shut my eyes and was ready for a good beating, but nothing happened.

**16. A Man working on an electric power mower plugs in the plug. (para. 8)**
A Man who's going to mow his lawn put the plug of an electric power mower into the socket.
work on: (phrasal verb) to be in the process of doing sth
More examples:
The boy's working on a math problem.
The director's working on a new movie.

**17. He turns on the switch, on and off, but nothing happens. (para. 8)**
on and off/off and on: starting, stopping, and starting again
eg     It rained off and on for a week.
        The man worked in a restaurant on and off.
        She's had headaches on and off for a couple of years.

**18. ... a Woman is seen dialing her phone. (para. 8)**
sb is seen doing sth: the passive form of "people see sb doing sth"
More examples:
The man was seen putting his hand into a woman's bag.
The girl was seen playing with her pet dog.

**19. Operator, something's wrong with the phone. (para. 9)**
Operator, my phone is not working properly.
operator: someone who works for a telephone company, whom you can call for help when you have problems with your phone
something's wrong with... / there's something wrong with...
More examples:
There seems to be something wrong with the computer, but I don't know what it is.
There is nothing seriously wrong with you. You just have a little cold.

**20. ... the power's off. (para. 10)**
There is no power, power is no longer connected.
off: (adv) especially of machines, electrical devices, lights, etc) not operating because it's not switched on
More examples:
The light was off, and the room was pitch dark
The heat was off and it was terribly cold.
on: (adv) the opposite of "off"

**21. I can't get anybody on the phone, either. (para. 11)**
I can't get anybody to answer the phone. 没人接电话。

**22. Phone won't work. (para. 13)**
The phone refuses to work.
This is another elliptical sentence, in which the definite article "the" is missing.

won't: here, not an auxiliary verb (助动词) indicating future action. It is a modal verb (情态动词) used to describe a situation that is continuing.
More examples:
He won't stop asking for permission to start a garden in prison. (=He keeps asking... / He refuses to stop asking...)
The door won't open. (=It's impossible to open the door.)
work: (of a machine or device) to do what it is supposed to do; to operate without failure
More examples:
Our air-conditioner is working beautifully.
This Xerox machine doesn't work. We have to get it fixed.

**23. I'll cut through the back yard... see if the power's still on on Cherry Street. I'll be right back. (para. 16)**
cut through (a place): to go through a place because it is the shortest route to another place
More example:
To get home before dark, the boy decided to cut through the wood.
I'll be right back: I'll be back in a minute.
the power is still on on Cherry Street: The first "on" goes with "is still", the second one with "Cherry Street".

**24. Doesn't make sense. (para. 17)**
There doesn't seem to be a good reason or explanation for all this.
(sth) make sense:
1) to have a clear meaning and be easy to understand
eg     The instructions don't make any sense at all.
2) to be a sensible thing to do
eg     It makes sense to leave early to avoid traffic jams.
3) there seems to be a good reason or explanation for sth
eg     Why did she turn down the job? It doesn't seem to make sense.

**25. Why should the power and the phone line go off all of a sudden? (para. 17)**
should: (modal verb) used after "why" when giving or asking the reason for sth
More examples:
Why should anyone want to eat dog meat?
Why shouldn't she buy an expensive bag if she can afford it?
all of a sudden: suddenly

**26. Maybe it's an electrical storm or something. (para. 18)**
or something: (informal) used to suggest another name, choice, etc that is not specified
More examples:
Sit down and let me get you a cup of tea or something?
Maybe we can go to the movies or something.

**27. Well, why don't you go downtown and check with the police? (para. 21)**
Why don't you/we do sth: a way of making a suggestion
More examples:
Why don't you ask Harry for help? He's something of computer expert.
Why don't we all sit down and have a cup of tea?
downtown: (chiefly US) the main or central part of a city or town; the part of a city or town where there are tall buildings, stores, offices, etc
check with sb: to ask sb; to talk with sb in order to get information, approval, etc, about sth
More examples:
You'd better check with your doctor to find out whether you can make this long trip.
I'll check with my lawyer and see what he has to say about this.

**28. A little power failure and right away we get all excited. (para. 21)**
As soon as anything goes wrong, we'll get nervous/make a fuss.
Pay attention to the structure of the sentence:
noun phrase + and + a clause indicating result
More examples:
A few nice words and he will do anything for you. (=If you say a few nice words, he will do anything for you.)
One step forward and you are dead! (=As soon as you take one step forward, you'll be dead.)
One more mistake and you are fired! (=If you make one more mistake, you'll be fired.)

**29. We'll get this all straightened out. (para. 23)**
straighten sth out: (phrasal verb) to solve a problem or to deal successfully with a confusing situation.
More examples:
It will take a while before the two countries can straighten out their differences.
We still need to straighten out a few things before we sign the agreement.

**30. Out of gas? (para. 25)**
Is the car out of gas? / Have you run out of gas? / You have no more gas?
out of: (a person or a thing) to no longer have sth
More examples:
We're out of sugar/coffee/salt, etc.
The grocery store's out of skim milk.
We must help those who are out of work.

**31. I just had it filled up. (para. 26)**
I had my car filled with gas a very short time ago.
fill (sth) up: (phrasal verb) to become full, or to make sth become full
eg     Our collection box (捐款箱) is filling up fast.
        Their eyes filled up with tears when the movie was over.

**32. It is just as if... as if everything had stopped. (para. 28)**
More examples of the use of "as if":
He looked as if he had never heard of it. (with a clause)
She opened her mouth as if to say something. (with an infinitive phrase)
He was standing there as if waiting for somebody. (with a present participle phrase)
The wolves were about to jump on the boy when Maheegun came, as if by magic, to the boy's rescue. (with a preposition phrase)

**33. We'd better walk downtown. (para. 28)**
We had better walk downtown.
had better: 1) to make a suggestion when the subject is "we"
eg     It's getting dark; we'd better take a taxi home.
2) to give advice if the subject is "you" and what you advise sb to do is for his/her own good
eg     You'd better stop smoking.
2) to make a threat if the subject is "you" and what you tell sb to do is for your own good
eg     You'd better give me your gun.

**34. It couldn't be the meteor. (para. 29)**
It was not likely to be the meteor.

**35. jerks his head (para. 34)**
moves his head suddenly and quickly

**36. Whoever was in that thing that came by overhead. (para. 37)**
Those who were in that UFO which came flying over our head.

whoever: no matter who
come by: to make a short visit to a place on one's way to somewhere else
overhead: (adv) above your head

**37. Whatever gave you that idea? (para. 40)**
What gave you that idea?
"Whatever" here is more emphatic than "what". It is often used to show anger or impatience.
More examples:
It can't be done? Whatever do you mean?
A policeman came? Whatever did he want?
Whatever is the matter with you?

**38. From outer space yet! (para. 43)**
(他) 还在说什么"来自外星"呢!

**39. ... that boy of yours ... (para. 43)**
This is more emphatic than "your boy". It is often used to express strong approval or disapproval, here disapproval.
More examples:
That daughter of yours is really sweet.
That coat of yours is dirty!
Look, I'm not at all interested in that car of yours!

**40. comic books (para. 43)**
(also comics AmE) magazines that tell children funny stories in pictures

**41. And this thing, being so close—why, there's no telling the sort of stuff it can do. (para. 46)**
being so close: as it was so close
why: (inter) used to show that you are surprised or annoyed
More examples:
And her son is so young. Why, he is not yet six.
I was so busy yesterday. Why, I didn't even have time to eat my meals.
There's no knowing/telling/saying: (informal) It is not possible to know what will happen.
More examples:
There's no telling how long the talks could drag on.
There's no telling what the voters will do in the next election.

**42. You might not even be able to get to town. (para. 49)**
might: used here to show sth is possible although you are not certain about it.
More examples:
You might be late if you walk there.
I don't know. She might like it.
You might have heard about it.

**43. And it wasn't until the ship landed that... (para. 51)**
一直到飞船着陆以后才......
More examples of "not... until":
It wasn't until this morning that I finally heard the news.
It wasn't until my mother passed away that I realized how much I loved her.
It wasn't until I met you that my life began to have a purpose.

**44. ... conscious of his parents staring at him... (para. 51)**
More examples of "conscious of sb doing sth":
All this time, I was conscious of somebody following me.
He was working so hard. He was not even conscious of somebody entering the room.

**45. That was the way they prepared things for the landing. (para. 55)**
the way sb does sth: the manner in which sb behaves or thinks, or in which sth happens
More examples:
That's the way we do things here.
I don't like the way he speaks to the students.
I didn't mind what he said, but the way he said it hurt me.

**46. There's laughter at this, but it's a laughter that comes from a desperate attempt to lighten the atmosphere. (para. 55)**
Hearing Tommy's words, some people laugh, not because they think the boy's remarks funny, but because they are terrified and try to reduce people's anxiety about the situation.

**47. ... got the same deal... (para. 56)**
got the same problem

**48. ... the car engine start up all by itself. (para. 58)**
... the car engine begins working with nobody operating it.
(all) by itself: (of an object) without a person making it work More examples:
The door seemed to open all by itself.
For a few minutes, the curtain opened and closed, on and off, all by itself.

**49. How come his car just started like that? (para. 60)**
how come: used to ask how or why sth has happened
Note: Unlike the wh-question, this does not require inversion (=any change) in the word order.
cf      Why didn't you go?
        How come you didn't go?

**50. And he never did come out... (para. 62)**
Drill students in the use of "do" in affirmative sentences for emphasis.
eg      —I thought you like fish.
        —I do like fish, but I'm too full
Substitutes:
I thought you also went to the party.
I thought you have a sister.
I thought your father worked in the Army.

**51. ... the general impression holds that... (para. 70)**
... most people here believe that...
hold: (formal) to have a particular opinion or belief

**52. different from us (para. 70)**
Note: "From" is the preposition generally used both in writing and speaking. "To" is common in spoken English. "Different than" is also accepted and used in American English.
Call students' attention to the importance of "adj + prep" collocations, such as different from, interested in, afraid of, fond of, sure of, busy with, good at, angry with, be full of, etc.

**53. ... fit that description... (para. 70)**
... like is the person Tommy describes—an alien from outer space sent to Maple Street to prepare for the landing
fit/match a/the description: to be like the description given by the police, witness, etc
More examples:
He doesn't fit the description, he is much too thin.
This is not the house my mother's looking for. It doesn't fit the description.

**54. practical joke (para. 71)**
a trick that is intended to give sb a surprise or shock, or to make them look stupid

**55. I just don't understand it... any more than any of you do! (para. 72)**
I don't understand why my car engine goes on and off as everybody else doesn't.
not any more than/no more than: used to emphasize that sb does not have a particular quality or would or does not do sth
More examples:
I don't earn any more than you do. Why do I have to pay more?
I don't want to go any more than you do. But somebody has to go there.
I don't like him any more than you do. But we have to help him.

**56. A couple of times... I'd come out here on the porch and I'd see... (para. 77)**
... I would come out here... and I would see...
would: used to say that sth happened often and regularly in the past
More examples:
Professor Li would always begin a lesson with a quiz.
Whenever he came to Beijing, he'd take an overnight train.

**57. She's making it up, anyway. (para. 78)**
make sth up: to invent sth, such as an excuse or a story, often in order to deceive
More examples:
Nobody believed him. He made up the whole story.
The boy made up an excuse to cut classes.

**58. There's a quiet that blankets the whole area... (para. 79)**
... the street has become very quiet...
Note: In the sentence "blankets" is used figuratively.
blanket: (v) to cover with or as if with a blanket
eg      New grass blankets the river banks.

**59. It doesn't seem right, though, keeping watch on them. (para. 80)**
though: (adv) in spite of what the woman says
keep (a) watch on sb: to watch sb carefully to make sure that they are safe or they do not do sth bad
More examples:
The police kept a close watch on a gang of drug dealers.
A nurse sat by the patient's bed and kept watch on him.

**60. Maybe under normal circumstances we could let it go by... (para. 81)**
Normally, we could tolerate and ignore all that and wouldn't say anything...
Here "it" refers to Goodman's habit of looking up at the sky in the small hours of the morning.

**61. Just stay where you are, Steve. (para. 82)**
Don't come near. / Don't move closer to my house.

**62. ... if anybody sets foot on my porch... (para. 82)**
set foot in/on a place: to go to a place, especially when there's sth special about you doing this
More examples:
Neil Armstrong made history when he set foot on the Moon in 1969.
He would never again set foot in the town which he had left in disgrace.

**63. You'd better watch who you're seen with, Steve! ... you aren't exactly above suspicion yourself. (para. 87)**
This is a warning to Steve that if he supports/speaks for Goodman, he would be suspected, too.
above/beyond suspicion: definitely not guilty of doing anything wrong
cf      under suspicion: believed to be possibly guilty of doing sth wrong
eg      After Pearl Harbor, her father was under suspicion simply because he had worked for a Japanese firm.

**64. From age eight and up! (para. 88)**
Every one of us living on Maple Street is under suspicion. Everyone who's eight years old or older.
age eight: (AmE) eight-year old

**65. self-appointed hanging judge (para. 91)**
self-appointed: behaving as if you had responsibility or authority but without having been chosen by other people
a hanging judge: a judge who is likely to give severe punishments to people who commit crimes

**66. I think we'd better keep our eye on you, too! (para. 92)**
keep our/your/an eye on sb: to keep (a) watch on sb (Refer to Note 59.)

**67. I think everything might as well come out now. (para. 93)**
We might soon know who the alien is that has been sent from outer space and hidden among us.
come out: (of sth that has been kept secret) to become known publicly
More examples:
The truth about what really happened came out finally.

**68. Go ahead, what's my wife said? Let's get it all out. (para. 95)**
Ok, tell us what my wife has said. Tell us all she's said.
go ahead: said to sb in order to give them permission to start to do sth
More examples:
1)      — Could I ask a couple of questions?
        — Sure, go ahead.
2)      — Could I go and get some writing paper? I'll be right back.
        — Certainly, go ahead.
get sth out (of sb): to persuade or force sb to give information, etc

**69. And then we might as well set up some kind of kangaroo court. (para. 95)**
Note Steve's sarcastic tone throughout paragraph 95. He uses various expressions of making suggestions, such as "Let's do sth", "might as well do sth", "How about...", etc. Actually he's pointing out that his neighbors are getting unreasonable, and they're turning into a mob.
might as well: used to make a suggestion when what you are suggesting seems to be the only sensible course of action, although you are not enthusiastic about it.
More examples:
I'm not sure what I should do, so I might as well go with the flow (=Do what most people do).
Anyway, you're here; you might as well stay.
If they don't get along, they might as well separate for some time.
kangaroo court: an unofficial court that punishes people illegally and unfairly

**70. How about a firing squad at dawn, Charlie, so we can get rid of all the suspects? (para. 95)**
firing squad: a group of soldiers who are ordered to shoot and kill a prisoner

**71. How come you're so dense all of a sudden? (para. 98)**
Steve is surprised that Charlie should become so unreasonable that he asks such personal questions. Steve's remarks also suggest that under normal circumstances Charlie is a friendly and reasonable person.

**72. a ham radio set (para. 99)**
a radio set operated by a ham—someone who sends and receives radio messages for fun. The word "ham" is often used before another noun, such as a ham radio, a ham radio operator, a ham radio station, etc. Obviously, Steve's radio set can both send and receive messages.

**73. You're standing here all set to find a scapegoat—all desperate to point a finger at a neighbor! (para. 102)**
all set to do sth: to be ready to do sth. It is an adjective phrase, used here as a subject complement.
More examples:
The team is all set to win the championship.
The little girl was all set to do it herself.
point a/the finger at sb, point an accusing finger at sb: to accuse or blame sb for sth bad that has happened
More examples:
Whenever something goes wrong, someone will point a finger at me.
It's no use pointing fingers at each other. Let's put our heads together to find a solution.

**74. Well now, look, friends, the only thing that's going to happen is that we'll eat each other up alive—(para. 102)**
If we keep blaming each other, we will get nothing straightened out, and we'll only destroy ourselves.
Here Steve speaks out the message of the teleplay. Have students locate other sentences/paragraphs that convey the message.
eat sb (up) alive: to be very angry with sb; to criticize sb very angrily; to defeat or destroy sb
More examples:
Once the truth comes out, they will eat us alive.
If they disclose more secret information, the military will eat them alive.
If I fail to meet the deadline for a paper again, Professor Liu will eat me alive.

**75. A figure suddenly materializes in the darkness. (para. 103)**
A person suddenly appears unexpectedly in the darkness. / Suddenly, in a strange way, people see the shape of a person in the darkness.

**76. In the silence we can hear the slow, measured footsteps on concrete... (para. 103)**
It is very quiet and we can hear the figure's slow, careful and controlled steps.

## （续）

**77. Will you people wise up? (para. 106)**

This is a rhetorical question. Steve is trying to urge Don Martin and some others to think and act sensibly and not to use a gun when they are not certain who the approaching figure is.
you: (informal) often used with a noun when people speak to someone or several people directly.
More examples:
Calm down, you girls!
You boys, come over.
Will you guys listen to reason?
wise up: to start to think and act in a more intelligently way

**78. You'd let whatever's out there walk right over us, wouldn't you? (para. 107)**

Charlie accuses Steve of not doing anything to protect people on Maple Street from the coming Monster.
whatever's out there: whatever's moving toward us whether it's an object, a person or a monster
walk (all) over sb: to defeat them very easily

**79. No! No! Please! (para. 118)**

The following stage direction describes how violent people on Maple Street become. They have turned into a mob attacking Charlie. Ironically, the person who's been most anxiously and madly searching for a scapegoat has now fallen victim to the madness of his neighbors.

**80. People in the crowd take this up and repeat the question aloud. (para. 127)**

People pick out a new scapegoat and begin to deal with Tommy who is now suspected of being the monster.

**81. Understand the procedure now? (para. 136)**

Question: What procedure?
The procedure of conquering/destroying each human community by causing the people to suspect one another and finally destroy themselves.

**82. Then I take it this place... this Maple Street... is not an exception. (para. 139)**

I suppose this Maple Street is not an exception.
I (can) take it (that)...: I suppose that...
More examples:
I take it that you've heard about Joe's divorce?
Can I take it you're not going to the party?

**83. By no means. (para. 140)**

I don't at all think this Maple Street is an exception
by no means: not at all, used as a way of emphasizing a negative statement
More examples:
The project is by no means a complete failure.
He's by no means an unsociable person.

**84. The tools of conquest do not necessarily come with bombs and explosions and fallout. (para. 141)**

Not necessarily: used to say that sth is possibly true but not definitely or always true
不一定，未必
eg  The more expensive things are not necessarily better.
fallout: the radiation that affects a particular place or area after a nuclear explosion has taken place 核辐射

**85. For the record, prejudices can kill and suspicion can destroy and a thoughtful, frightened search for a scapegoat has fallout all of its own for the children... and the children yet unborn... (para. 141)**

(just) for the record: used to emphasize a point that you are making, so that the person you are speaking to takes notice
eg  I don't like his attitude, and, for the record, he would be the last person I would turn for help.
Fallout here means "the bad results of a situation or an action" (后果，余波).
Here the author is pointing out that it is human beings' own weaknesses, such as suspicion and prejudice, rather than mass-destructive weapons that destroy themselves. As in the case on Maple Street, when people are mindlessly looking for a scapegoat among themselves because they are frightened by the disruption of their normal life, they turn into their own worst enemies. Such human weaknesses will have consequences on children of this generation and the generations that follow.

**86. And the pity of it is... that these things cannot be confined to... The Twilight Zone! (para. 141)**

Unfortunately for us, what happens on Maple Street is not just a made-up story for a TV series; it is actually happening in reality.

### III Translation of Text A

恶魔降临枫树街

罗德·塞林

第一幕

[枫树街，典型的美国小镇一个住宅区里的一条街道，十分安静，两旁绿树成荫。房屋的前门都有门廊，人们可以坐在那儿隔着自家草坪与邻居聊天。史蒂夫·布兰德在给停在门前的自家汽车抛光，他的邻居唐·马丁靠在车的挡泥板上看着他抛光。古德休姆公司的一位员工骑着自行车疾驶激溅，他停下车，向几个小孩疾溅激泼。两个女人在房前草坪上聊天，一个男人在给自家院子里的草浇水。
此刻，一个叫汤米的男孩抬起头来听天空传来的巨响，一道闪光扫过他的面庞，接着划过这条街道，扫过草地、门廊和屋顶。最后消失了。一直在抛光汽车的史蒂夫站起身子一言不发，两眼盯着天空。他看了看同街对面的邻居唐·马丁]
史蒂夫：那是什么？是流星吗？
唐：看上去像，我可没听到流星坠落的声音，你听到了吗？
史蒂夫：没有，除了一声巨响，我什么也没听到。
布兰德太太：（从门里）史蒂夫？出什么事了？
史蒂夫：亲爱的，我猜是流星，来得可真近，是不是？
布兰德太太：太近了！
（人们站在各自的门廊里，一边观察一边低声交谈着。观众可以看到一个男子正在自家门前的门廊里安装灯泡，然后从凳子上下来，按开关，但灯没有亮。另一个男子正准备用电动割草机修整草坪，他把插头插进了插座，打开电源，打开了叉叉，来回几次，割草机还没有动静。透过一家门廊的窗户，观众能看到一个女人正在拨打电话。）
女人：喂，喂，接线员，我家电话拨不通了，接线员！
（布兰德太太从屋里走到门廊。）
布兰德太太：（喊道）史蒂夫，停电了！炉子上本来正烧着汤呢，突然停电了。
女人：我这儿也一样，电话也打不通了，话机好像失灵了。
声音甲：停电了。
声音乙：电话不通了。
声音丙：收音机什么也收不到。
声音丁：我的割草机不动了，一点儿也不动了。
（皮特·范·霍恩，一个又高又瘦的男子，站在他的房前。）
范·霍恩：我从后院的窗户看……看看樱桃街那儿是不是还有电，我一会儿就回来。
史蒂夫：这没道理啊，电与电话怎么突然都不通了？ 
唐：没准是雷暴或是类似的气象问题吧？
查理：不像呀！天这么蓝，没有云，没有闪电，什么也没有，怎么可能是雷暴呢？
女人：收音机一点儿信号也没有了，连手提的都没有信号。
查理：要不你到镇上问一下警察吧？不过，他们也许会觉得我们疯了或怎么着，一次小小的停电事故就让我们大惊小怪起来。
史蒂夫：查理，这可不仅仅是停电事故，如果是的话，我们从手提收音机上还是能收听到广播的。
（人们对此议论纷纷，史蒂夫向他的汽车走去。）
史蒂夫：我到镇上跑一趟，我们会把事情搞清楚的。（史蒂夫进了车，转动了车钥匙，发动机没动静，他又从车里出来了。）
史蒂夫：真不明白是怎么回事，刚才车还好好的——
唐：没汽油了？
史蒂夫：（摇头）我刚加满了油。
女人：那这是怎么回事？
查理：好像……好像什么都停止了。（接着他转向史蒂夫）那我们就走吧。
史蒂夫：好吧，查理。（他转过身往身后看了看那辆车。）不太可能是流星闹的。流星可引起不起这种事。
（他和查理相互看了一眼，接着他俩离开人群。一个表情严肃的男孩子汤米想阻拦他俩。）
汤米：布兰德先生……您最好别去！
史蒂夫：为什么？
汤米：他们不想让你们去。
（史蒂夫和查理相视一笑，史蒂夫扭头看了看那男孩。）
史蒂夫：谁不想让我们去？
汤米：（往遥远的地平线方向猛地甩了下头）他们！
史蒂夫：他们？
查理：他们是谁？
汤米：（十分急切地）就是刚才从我们头顶上飞过的那玩意的家伙。我觉得他们不想让我们离开这里。
（史蒂夫走近男孩，在他面前蹲下，膝盖着地，尽量使自己的语气柔和，他伸出手，握住那男孩的胳膊。）
史蒂夫：你指的是什么？你刚才说的话是什么意思？
汤米：他们不想让我们离开。所以他们把所有的东西都关闭了。
史蒂夫：那你的根据是什么呢？你怎么会有那样的想法？
女人：诸位，你们听说过这么离奇的事情吗？
汤米：（固执己见地）我读过的每一个有关天外飞船着陆的故事里都是这么说的。
女人：（冲着男孩的妈妈萨莉）还说什么从天外来的呢！萨莉，你还是赶紧叫你那宝贝儿子上床睡觉去吧。他要不是漫画书看多了，就是这种电影看多了！
萨莉：汤米，到妈妈这儿来，快别讲那些话了。
史蒂夫：汤米，跟你妈妈回家去吧！我们很快就回来，到时候你就会明白了，那根本不是什么飞船，不过是流星一类玩意儿了。（他转向人群，尽量让自己的声音听起来乐观，虽然他并不那样觉得。）流星像太阳黑子一样，有时也会惹起一些稀奇古怪的事。
唐：可不全是嘛，太阳黑子能干扰全世界的无线电接收。刚才飞过去的那个玩意，飞得这么近——真说不准它会搞出什么乱。 （他瞟了瞟嘴唇，忐忑不安地笑了笑。）去吧，查理。你和史蒂夫到镇上去问问是不是这么回事。
（史蒂夫和查理继续沿着人行道走着，人们默默地注视着他们，汤米盯着他们，咬着嘴唇，终于又大声叫了起来。）
汤米：布兰德先生！
（那两个男人停住了脚步。）
汤米：布兰德先生……请不要离开这儿。
（史蒂夫和查理又一次停下了脚步，转向那男孩。众人开始低声吼嚷起来，显出愤怒与担心。）
汤米：你们也许根本就到不了镇上，那些故事里是这么说的，谁也走不了，除了——
史蒂夫：除了谁？
汤米：除了那些他们事先派来的人，他们长得跟人类一模一样。直到飞船降落的时候，他们才——（男孩突然又停下了，他意识到父母在盯着他，人群也顿时变得鸦雀无声。）
萨莉：汤米，快别说了，孩子，别那么说了——
男人甲：这孩子怎么能说这种胡话……我们这些人干嘛要站在这儿听他胡说八道，噢，这可是我听过的最怪异的怪事了。
（史蒂夫走近那男孩。）
史蒂夫：说下去，汤米，他们事先派来的人怎么了？
汤米：他们准备飞船着陆时都是这么做的，他们派来和人类长得一模一样的人……不过他们不是人类。
（此刻人群中发出了笑声，但这种笑声的出发点是想要拼命缓解紧张的气氛。）
查理：（不安地触摸着自己的腮帮子）不知道樱桃街的情况是不是跟这儿一样。
（他朝那条街的方向望去。）皮特·范·霍恩到底在哪儿？他还没回来吗？
（突然人们听到了汽车发动机的声音。莱斯·古德曼坐在车的方向盘后面。）
萨莉：莱斯，车能发动起来了？
（古德曼从车里出来了，摇摇头。）
古德曼：不能。
（他走向人群，突然又停下了。在他身后，汽车的发动机居然自己发动起来了。古德曼猛地转过身，盯着车，他睁大了眼睛，向汽车跑过去，所有的人都瞪大眼睛看着那辆车。）
男人甲：他居然把车发动起来了，他把车发动起来了！
女人：他的车怎么能发动起来呢？
萨莉：车是它自己发动起来的呢，他本人没在汽车边上，这汽车全是自己发动起来的。
（唐走向人群走去；在距人群几英尺远的地方他停了下来，看着古德曼的汽车，然后又回头看人群。）
唐：他就没有出来看天空飞过的那玩意，他压根就不感兴趣。（他转而看大家的脸。）为什么？他为什么不跟着大伙一块儿出来看一看呢？
查理：他一直是个人怪人，他跟全家都怪怪的。
唐：咱们问一问他，怎么样？
（人群突然走向古德曼的家。）
史蒂夫：等等……等一会儿！大伙别冲动！
（众人仿佛停顿了片刻，然后，更加平静地、缓慢地穿过马路。古德曼孤身一人站在那儿，面对众人。）
古德曼：我也不明白，我怎么试都没能把车发动起来，你们不是都看见了嘛。
（就在这时候，发动机又突然自动停了，就跟它刚发动起来一样，人群中开始出现了恐慌的喃喃声。）
唐：也许你能跟我们解释解释，这条街上什么都不运转了，灯不亮了，电停了，收音机没有信号了，一切都不动了，就一辆车除外——你的车。
（众人明白了唐的意思，他们的交头接耳声转变成了大声的呼喊，齐声要求采取行动。）
古德曼：等等。别走过来，你们所有的人都别靠近我。我确实有一辆自己发动起来的汽车，很怪，是吧？这我承认，难道因此我就变成罪犯？我自己也不知道那汽车为什么会自己发动起来，可它就这样动起来了！
（这番话语使众人停了下来，古德曼退着走上了台阶，然后停下来面对着人群。）
古德曼：史蒂夫，这到底是怎么回事？
史蒂夫：（平静地）大伙好像认为有哪家一家人可能不是我们想象的那样。他们是天外来的恶魔或什么的，跟大伙不一样。你知道在枫树街有谁可能符合这个描述吗？
古德曼：究竟是怎么回事？是恶作剧吗？
（突然汽车的发动机又自个儿转动起来了，转了一会儿又停了下来。众人又有反应了。）
古德曼：我的车一会发动，一会又停下，这就让我成了一个罪犯，是不是？（他环视众人的面孔。）我也不明白，跟你们所有人一样不明白！（他舔了舔嘴唇，从这张脸望向那张脸。）听我说，你们都了解我，我们家在这里已经住了五年了，就住在这所房子里。我们跟你们没有什么不同！
女人：如果真是这样的话，莱斯·古德曼，你解释解释为什么……（她突然停下了。）
古德曼：（轻声地）解释什么？
史蒂夫：（插入谈话）呃，咱们还是别说那些吧——
查理：说下去，让她说下去。什么事？叫他解释什么？
女人：（有点儿不情愿地）嗯，……我有时夜里睡得很晚，有几次……我在门厅看到古德曼先生站在他的屋前……往天上看……，好像……好像在等待什么东西。
古德曼：她简直是疯了，这事我可以解释，请听我说……我真的可以解释这事。她是在胡编乱造。
（他向人群迈出了一步，众人后退，他跟着在这些人的后面走下台阶，众人继续后退。突然之间，他感到被彻底地孤立了，他看上去像个陷入敌对人群的重重包围的人。）
第二幕
[观众可以看到几户人家房屋里的蜡烛，但仍然没有电，这条街笼罩在一片寂静之中，站在周围的人们低声耳语，也只有他们的耳语声才对这种寂静有些许干扰。查理瞪着双眼看着对面古德曼家的房子]
萨莉：（有些胆怯地）不过，这样盯着他们好像不太对头。他说得对呀，他是我们的邻居。可不是嘛，自打他们一家搬到这儿，我就认识了埃塞尔·古德曼，我们一直是好朋友——
查理：那证明不了什么。谁要是一清早就盯着天空看……嗯，那个人肯定有问题。要是在平时，我们也许可以不管，不过现在可是非常时期呀。
（几米以外的范·霍恩从自家门厅的台阶走了下来，来到古德曼家门口，在台阶下停了下来。古德曼站在那儿，古德曼太太站在她的身后，惊恐万分。）
古德曼：打住，别再往前迈，史蒂夫，我们不想招惹麻烦，不过这次谁要是迈进我们的门厅，他就会自我麻烦！
史蒂夫：莱斯，听我说——
古德曼：我已经跟你们这些人解释过了，我有时夜里睡不好觉，我就起床出来走走，看看天空，我是看星星！
古德曼太太：确实就是这么回事，这整个事情……简直荒唐。
史蒂夫：（点头）确实是这样——确实荒唐！
查理的声音：（在马路对面尖声叫）史蒂夫，劝你还是留神你和谁一伙，这些事我们没弄清楚之前你也脱离不了干系。
史蒂夫：（冲着他急速转过身来）还有你，查理，还有我们当中八岁以上的所有人，都脱离不了干系。
女人：我想知道的是我们该干嘛？难道整夜就这么站在这儿？
查理：没有别的办法。
史蒂夫：（提高了嗓门）查理，你倒是有件事可以做，你可以闭上你的嘴，别在这里充当自封的判刑严厉的法官，回家去上床睡觉大觉，别在这儿瞎扯。
查理：你这话听起来是想让事情快点发生吧，史蒂夫，看来我们也得监视你呀！
唐：我想现在真相也快大白了，（他转身面向史蒂夫）你老婆可说了不少你的那些怪事呢！
查理：（明白了这话的意思，他的眼睛睁得大大的）说下去，他老婆都说了些什么？
（史蒂夫从马路上面对他俩走了过去。）
史蒂夫：说吧，我老婆什么了？全说出来。我们可以从这条街的每个男人、女人、孩子的身上挑出每一个与众不同的习惯，然后私设法庭来定罪，在天亮时建立一支行刑队，把嫌疑人都干掉，怎么样，查理？
唐：史蒂夫，没有必要这么动气吧！噢，我不过是……嗯，……我拉你说你经常夜里在地下室花好几个小时折腾一种收音机。
（此时史蒂夫已经走到众人跟前，挑战性地站在众人边上。）
查理：说呀，史蒂夫，你用的是什么什么样的无线电收发设备？用它跟谁联系？你跟谁通话？
史蒂夫：查理，我真感到惊讶！你怎么突然变得这么浑了。（停顿片刻）谁跟我通话？跟外星的恶灵呀！跟我通话的是长着三个头、有绿色皮肤的外星人呀！他们乘坐像流星一样的玩意飞到了这里！
布兰德太太：史蒂夫！史蒂夫！别……（恐惧地环顾四周，向众人走去。）他用的不过是业余无线电设备。好多人都有，我可以拿给大家伙看，就在地下室里。
史蒂夫：（急速向她转过身来）什么也别给他们看！他们要是想进屋搜查，那就拿出搜查许可证来。
查理：喂，伙计，你不可能犯……
史蒂夫：（打断他）别来教训我，告诉我谁是危险分子，谁不是；谁安全，谁威胁他人的安全。（他转身面对人群，喊了起来。）你们都跟他一伙，你们所有人！你们呆在这儿，一心想找出一个替罪羊，个个都急于指责一个街坊！好，朋友们，想一想，这样做最后只能让我们相互指责，自取灭亡……
（查理突然抓住他的胳膊，他嘎然停下。）
查理：（压低了嗓音）我们的结局还不只是这样呢。
（此刻，一个黑影忽然在夜幕中出现，寂静中，黑影慢慢向众人走过来，我们可以听见踏在水泥地上，缓慢、慎重、又有节奏的脚步声。一个女人压着嗓子发出一声喊叫。）
汤米：（惊叫地叫起来）妖魔来了！妖魔来了！
（众人集体后退，目不转睛地看着黑影在黑暗中逼近。唐·马丁携带一支猎枪加入了他们的行列，他举起了枪。）
唐：没准用得着。
史蒂夫：猎枪？（他从唐的手里夺过了猎枪。）大伙别犯糊涂！猎枪有什么用如果是用来对付——？
（查理一把从史蒂夫手中夺过了枪。）
查理：住嘴，史蒂夫。你再说我们就该进棺材了！你想让来的那家伙一举灭了我们，是不是？我们几个是决不会让你得逞的！
（他晃动了手中的枪，指向了人行道。黑影继续向众人走来，查理慢慢地举起了枪，待黑影来得更近时，他扣动了扳机，寂静中枪声响起，黑影轻轻叫了一声，随即倒下，开始双膝跪地，后来趴下了。唐、查理和史蒂夫跑了过去。史蒂夫最先到，他把那人的身体翻了过来，众人围在四周。）
史蒂夫：（慢慢抬起头来）是皮特·范·霍恩。
唐：（压低了嗓音）皮特·范·霍恩！他刚才去邻街看看那儿是不是也停电了。
女人：查理，你杀了他。你开枪打死了他。
查理：（环顾周围众人的脸，目光惊恐。）但……但我当时不知道那黑影是谁，我，我当然不知道那黑影是谁。他从黑暗里走来，我怎么会知道那是谁？（他抓住了史蒂夫）史蒂夫，你是了解我为什么开枪的！我怎么知道他不是妖魔？（他抓住了唐。）
我们都被同一样东西吓坏了。我只不过是在……想保护自己的家呀！仅此而已！（他朝下看着那具尸体，好像已经失去控制。）我不知道来的是我们认识的人呀，我不知道……
（众人突然都鸦雀无声。马路对面一座房子里的所有的灯都亮了起来。）
女人：（声音很低）查理，……查理，……刚才你家的灯全亮了。为什么灯都亮了？
唐：查理，你怎么解释？为什么只有你家的灯亮着？
古德曼：我也想问问这是怎么回事。
（众人都盯着查理，无言。）
古德曼：查理，你刚才杀人的动作倒是挺麻利的，你也很着急告诉大伙该提防谁。也许，你是不得不杀人，皮特刚才没准是要对我们说点什么的，要告诉我们究竟要小心我们中间的哪个人——
（查理后退，躲开众人，眼中闪现出惊恐的神色。）
查理：不是这样的，不是……我也不知道为什么我家的灯都亮了，我保证我不知道。有人开玩笑，还是什么别的原因吧。
（他攥在史蒂夫身上，史蒂夫一把抓住他，使他转过身。）
史蒂夫：开玩笑？查理，人行道上可躺着个死人，是你杀了他！这件事你觉得像个玩笑？
（查理甩开了史蒂夫，一边尖叫，一边跑向自己的家。）
查理：不！不！求你们了！
（一个男子离开了人群，跑去追赶查理，这人把他绊倒，扑在他身上，其他人向他跑过来，查理站了起来，摆脱了那男子的控制，跳进了自己家的门厅。人群中有人扔了一块石头砸碎了他身边的一扇窗户，玻璃碎片从他身旁飞过，几块碎片将他划伤，他站立在原地，鲜血从他面颊的一处伤口滴下。他的妻子摆脱了人群一头扑进他的怀抱。观众可以看到人群向他家门厅逼近。）
声音甲：肯定是他。
声音乙：就是他。
声音丙：我们得抓住他。
（又有一块石头落在门厅里。查理把妻子推到身后，面对着众人。）
查理：听我说，听我说，我向大伙保证……不是我……不过我不知道是谁，我对你们发誓，我知道那恶魔就在这儿。
唐：好吧，查理，我们洗耳恭听。
男人乙：（叫道）说吧，查理，告诉我们吧！
查理：是……是那个男孩子，是汤米。
莎莉：简直荒唐，他只不过是个孩子。
女人：不过他什么都知道，而且就他一个人知道！是他告诉我们那些事的，要不然我们怎么会知道？
（众人领会了这话的意思，大声地重复这个问题。）
声音甲：他是怎么会知道那些事的？
男人乙：又是谁告诉他这些事的？
男人丙：叫他回答我们的问题。
（莎莉拉起汤米就跑，众人紧跟，开始是快走，后来紧跟母子俩跑了起来。此时查理家的灯又都灭了，另一家的灯全亮了。）
男人：（喊道）不是那孩子……是鲍勃·韦弗家。
女人：不对，是唐·马丁家。
查理：我说是那孩子。
唐：是查理，就是他。
（意见不一的人齐声喊叫起来，相互指责，高声嚷嚷。屋里的灯忽而亮起，忽而熄灭。）

第二场

（一艘飞船在黑暗下停在附近的地里，光柱从一扇敞开的舱门射了出来，显现出两个身影。）
身影甲：现在你明白这个程序了吧？只须让他们的一些机器——收音机、电话机、割草机不转动……，让他们在黑暗中过上几个小时，然后坐着等着看结果吧。
身影乙：结果都一样吗？
身影甲：差异甚微。他们查找他们认为是最危险的敌人，然后发现就是他们自己。
身影乙：那我就说这样可以认为……这条枫树街……并不是个例外。
身影甲：绝对不是。他们的世界里到处都是枫树街，我们可以从一条街到另一条街，让他们自我消灭，从一处到另一处……从一处到另一处……再从一处到另一处。

第三幕

征服不一定伴随着炸弹、爆炸和核辐射而来。有些武器仅仅是思想、态度和偏见，只存在于人们的头脑中。事实上，偏见可以杀人，猜疑可以毁灭，出于恐惧而不加思考地寻找替罪羊，对现在和未来的孩子们来说，都是一场灾难。遗憾的是……这些东西不仅仅局限于……《阴阳魔界》！

### IV Keys to Exercises

#### Lead-in

2. In reading a play/drama, first we should learn about the setting or background (where/when), and to get to know the characters and their relationships with each other, then we must find out what is happening (plot), and understand how the drama leads to the climax or how the dramatic conflict is solved.

Language in a play is mostly dialogues with the use of colloquial and idiomatic expressions as well as elliptical sentences.

#### Language focus

1

These compound adjectives are formed by "nouns/numerals + -ed", different from those formed by "nouns + past participles," such as tree-lined, self-appointed, snow-covered.

2

In English, some words are both nouns and verbs, but stress differently. When a two-syllable word is used as a noun, the stress is usually on the first syllable. When it's used as a verb, however, the stress is usually on the second syllable.

3

Verb phrases:

come by, come out, shut off, straighten out, pick up, cut in, make up, keep watch on, set foot on, keep one's eye on sb., let out, wise up, watch out for, throw oneself into sb's arms, move in, move in on some people, take up, moves down the street, work on sth., check with sb., talk sb. into the grave, bump against sb., back away, go/turn/switch on/off

(Note: This play can serve as a good example of how various prepositions and adverbs can be used with common verbs. Take a careful study of them.)

4

Verb + noun collocations

park/polish the car, ride a bicycle, water the lawn, exchange a look, bite lips, lighten the atmosphere, keep distance, pull the trigger, make sense/a joke, dial her phone, jerk his head, blanket the whole area, raise his voice, protect my home, tackle him, smash a window, raise Cain, rub his jaw, get the same deal, do no good, grab his arm

5

1) Why should the power and the phone line go off all of a sudden? (para. 17)

("Should" is used after "why" when giving or asking the reason for sth.)

2) A little failure and right away we get all excited. (para. 21)

("And" is used to introduce a clause indicating result.)

3) ..., a woman is seen dialing her phone. (para. 8)

(Passive voice of "v + complex object")

#### Reading Comprehension

1

(1) a tremendous roar;
(2) have stopped;
(3) monsters;
(4) just like people;
(5) starts by itself;
(6) keep watch on;
(7) shoots;
(8) mob;
(9) accusing/pointing their fingers at;
(10) their most dangerous enemy

2

1. Maybe it is caused by a thunderstorm or something else.

("or something" is used to suggest another name, choice, etc. that is not specified.)

2. How can you say "from outer space" It's nonsense! Sally, you'd better put your son to bed.

("Yet" here is used for emphasis. The woman ridiculed Tommy, because she thought his claim was fanciful.)

3. They disrupt radio reception at different places in the world.

4. Most people seem to believe that your family are not what you appear to be, that you might be monsters from outer space.

(hold: to have a particular opinion or belief)

5. ... you yourself are not definitely innocent (you are guilty).

6. You could quit walking around as if you have the power or authority, which you don't, to hand out an unreasonably harsh sentence to a criminal, stop pretending and go to bed.

7. How about at dawn we have a group of soldiers to shoot and kill all the suspects, Charlie?

8. The shape of a person appears vaguely and gradually in the darkness.

9. What happens on Maple Street is not an isolated case. The weaknesses of the human being revealed here are commonly seen around the world.

3

1. The story takes place on Maple Street. The author stresses that Maple Street is in a typical American town, so the residents on Maple Street are typical of the masses in the US. What happens on Maple Street could happen in any community of the kind. Weaknesses of the residents on Maple Street are to be found in the humanity.

2. There was a power cut. All cars and electronic devices were dead, which was unusual and caused a general alarm among the residents. At the beginning, they thought it was a power failure or an electrical storm, and some suggested that it might be caused by meteor or sun spots, but no explanation satisfied all.

3. Tommy the boy. He had a guess based on the comic books he read. Under normal circumstances no one would believe him. But things were simply too weird to be explained by common sense or science. Any absurd idea seemed to make sense under an unusual circumstance. That Goodman's car started all by itself suggested the existence of supernatural force in the neighborhood, and the residents began to lose their grip.

4. People began to point their fingers at each other. Goodman was the first suspect. The next was Steve. No one was clear of suspicion in the end. They were out of their mind and unable to reach any reasonable decision.

5. The aliens from outer space cut off power and started the chaos in the community. Tommy, Steve, Don and Goodman, all were to blame. Mobs are not peaceful because in an excited crowd, conscious personality disappears and individuals are unable to make independent judgment. Sentiments and acts are contagious and people are easily incited and become violent. Besides, it's safe to follow the crowd. Staying sensible and trying to reason with the angry crowd could invite trouble and harm as is the case with Steve.

6. Yes. We're our own worst enemy. Fear, suspicion and prejudice will let loose monsters in ourselves. Students are encouraged to find examples in daily life and in history.

7. Residents on Maple Street think that aliens from outer space are monsters. However, monsters existed among the residents themselves. Suspicion and distrust of one another, eagerness to find a scapegoat, prejudice, and herd instinct had turned them into monsters.

Note: Beware of the differences between English and Chinese. In English, we say "a book," "a fish," "a pig," "a horse," "a banana," "an apple," "a star," etc. In Chinese, we have to say "一本书", "一条/尾鱼", "一头猪", "一匹马", "一根香蕉", "一个苹果", "一颗星", etc. There are no simple rules.

4

1. I guess it was a meteor, Honey. It came awful close, didn't it? (subjects)

2. The electricity's off. (definite article "the")

3. I can't get a thing on the radio. (subject)

4. It doesn't make sense. (subject)

5. His car just started all by itself. (subject and main verb)

6. What do you say if we ask him? (conjunction)

7. There're no lights, no power, no radio working. (introductory "there" and verb, noun qualifier)

8. It seems that the general impression holds that maybe the people in one family aren't what we think they are. (introductory "it," which stands for the subject "the general impression... what we think they are.")

9. They're monsters from outer space or something. (subject and main verb)

10. They're different from us. (subject and main verb)

6

1. We'd better watch out for any signs of bird flu. The sooner the better.

2. Did you say that it is going to snow heavily tomorrow according to the weather forecast? If that is the case, we might as well go this afternoon.

3. There's no telling what might happen in the long run if more and more young people are determined to have only one child or none at all.

4. How was I supposed to know all those things? I wasn't even born yet.

5. A little quarrel, and you start talking about divorce. Don't be childish, differences between a husband and wife are not difficult to straighten out.

6. I take it that the general medical opinion holds that washing hands and wearing masks can prevent viruses from spreading effectively.

7. Our rent for this quarter is due next Friday. You go and withdraw some money from the bank. Our landlady prefers cash as usual.

8. With the rise of online shopping, many people have not set foot inside a shopping mall for quite some time.

9. A discussion is an exchange of knowledge; an argument, an exchange of ignorance.

10. Man's capacity for justice makes democracy possible, but man's inclination to injustice makes democracy necessary. (Reinhold Niebuhr)

7

Student 1: Hey! Shall we organize a trip somewhere as a class one of these days?

Student 2: Good idea. Let's go to the Summer Palace. I cannot see enough of it. We can go boating on the lake or something.

Student 1: I cannot speak for everybody because I have been brought up in Beijing. But if I were you, I would go outside Beijing. You could go to the Summer Palace anytime.

Student 2: Good idea! Why don't we go to Inner Mongolia? I'd like to see the grasslands and learn to ride a horse.

Student 1: That's a bit too far away. Besides, there's always the budget limit. I suggest we go up north someplace near the Miyun Reservoir, where there is a combination of a lot of water and beautiful mountains. What do you say?

Student 2: I am all for it. But how can we get there? We could not go that far by bike or walking.

Student 1: I think we'd better rent a bus from a tourist company for the whole trip. That should give us a lot of freedom. As for the meals, what would you advise us to do?

Student 2: How about taking our food with us? I have always wanted to go picnicking together some day.

Student 1: It's pretty romantic. But you can't have picnics for two days. My proposal is to find a farm restaurant. I hear they offer wonderful meals at very reasonable prices.

Student 2: Fall is the best season in Beijing. We might as well take this trip as soon as possible. How about we make it next weekend?

1. Wang Ying can't be in the library now, for I saw her heading for the canteen a moment ago.

2. However hard I tried/ Although I tried hard/ Hard as I tried, the jeep would not stop; it kept sliding downhill.

3. It is a private matter between the young couple. You'd better not to interfere with it.

4. Why don't we all sit down and have a cup of tea? There is no telling when everybody will arrive with this heavy snow.

5. The conference ends on Friday, and I hear that our return tickets won't be available until Sunday evening. We might as well do some sightseeing during the long weekend. What do you say?

6. —What's the matter with Lin? It reminded her only yesterday to meet us here at eight. It's now half past, but she hasn't arrived yet.
—She is always punctual. I'm afraid something might have happened to her.

7. The villagers were careful to keep their distance from them at first, because experience had told them whoever had guns in their hands would not do them any good, but by and by, they found these soldiers were different. They would do whatever they could to help them.
